Proof of Zamolodchikov conjecture for semi-classical conformal blocks on the torus
Harini Desiraju, Promit Ghosal, Andrei Prokhorov

Abstract
. In 1986, Zamolodchikov conjectured an exponential structure for the semi-classical limit of conformal blocks on a sphere. This paper provides a rigorous proof of the analog of Zamolodchikov conjecture for Liouville conformal blocks on a one-punctured torus, using their probabilistic construction and show the existence of a positive radius of convergence of the semi-classical limit. As a consequence, we obtain a closed form expression for the solution of the Lamé equation, and show a relation between its accessory parameter and the classical action of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model evaluated at specific values of the solution.



1.
Introduction

Conformal field theory (CFT), a subclass of quantum field theory
,
provides a framework for constructing random functions on Riemannian manifolds, which transform covariantly under conformal (i.e., angle-preserving) mappings. Since the groundbreaking work by Belavin, Polyakov, and Zamolodchikov (BPZ) in 1984
[
7
]
, two-dimensional (2D) CFT has evolved into a major area of theoretical physics. It has significant applications in 2D statistical physics, string theory and has profound implications for mathematics. See
[
60
]
for a review and references therein.

An important subclass of conformal field theories (CFTs) is known as Liouville CFTs (LCFTs). These CFTs are characterized by a parameter called the central charge, denoted as
c
, which for LCFTs lies in the range

c\in[25,\infty)
. LCFT originated from Polyakov’s work on two-dimensional quantum gravity and bosonic string theory in 1981
[
56
]
. It was rigorously constructed using the path integral formalism of quantum field theory, initially on the sphere in
[
15
]
and subsequently on other Riemann surfaces in
[
16
,
31
,
27
]
. We refer the reader to
[
58
]
for a review.

The paper by Belavin, Polyakov, and Zamolodchikov in 1984 introduced a schematic approach known as the conformal bootstrap. This program aims to obtain explicit expressions for the correlation functions of a given two-dimensional CFT by expressing them in terms of its three-point sphere correlation functions and specific power series called conformal blocks. These conformal blocks are entirely determined by the Virasoro algebra, which encodes
local conformal symmetries. Notably, the central charge parametrizes conformal blocks of a particular CFT.

Recently, there has been rapid progress in rigorous approaches to CFT using probability, notably by
[
15
,
16
,
27
,
31
]
. Our starting point in this paper is a construction of Liouville conformal blocks on one-point torus introduced by
[
24
]
using rigorous path integral formulation based on probabilistic ideas. Their construction is via the Gaussian multiplicative chaos (GMC) measure,
a random measure defined by exponentiating the Gaussian free field (see e.g.
[
16
,
8
,
5
]
). This paper explores the semi-classical limit of the probabilistic representation of Liouville conformal blocks, focusing on the case of torus with a single marked point.
We consider the flat torus
\mathbb{T}_{\tau}
characterized by the modular parameter
\tau
, and the nome
q:=e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau}
.
The
1
-point toric correlation function of LCFT, rigorously constructed in
[
16
]
,
has the form
\langle e^{\alpha\Phi(0)}\rangle_{\tau}
, where
\langle\ldots\rangle_{\tau}
is the average over the random field
\Phi

for LCFT on
\mathbb{T}_{\tau}
and
\alpha
is called the vertex insertion weight. Conformal bootstrap formula for torus which is recently shown in
[
26
, Theorem 9]
expresses
\langle e^{\alpha\Phi(0)}\rangle_{\tau}
via one point toric conformal block
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
,

(1.1)

\displaystyle\langle e^{\alpha\Phi(0)}\rangle_{\tau}=\int_{\mathbb{R}}C_{\gamma}(\alpha,Q-\mathbf{i}P,Q+\mathbf{i}P)|\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)|^{2}dP,

where the parameter
\gamma
dictates the central charge through the relation

(1.2)

\displaystyle c=1+6Q^{2}\in(25,\infty),

\displaystyle Q:=\frac{\gamma}{2}+\frac{2}{\gamma}\in(2,\infty).

Here
C_{\gamma}(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3})
is the Liouville 3-point correlation function, whose exact description is given by the Dorn-Otto-Zamolodchikov-Zamolodchikov (DOZZ) formula, which was first proposed in
[
20
,
70
]
and later proved in
[
35
]
. It is worthwhile to note that the conformal bootstrap for sphere is recently proven in
[
35
]
and
[
26
]
showed it for other geometry including torus. Finally we note that
\gamma\to 0
is equivalent to
c\to\infty
, the limit of interest in this paper.

In a recent work,
[
24
, Theorem 1.1]
introduced a probabilistic description of
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
using the GMC measure. This probabilistic definition of
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
is shown to match with the formula as a power series in
q
of the same (defined via Virasoro algebra in
[
7
]
) through rigorously proving the Zamolodchikov recursion (as proposed in
[
72
,
73
,
28
]
).
In fact,
[
24
]
showed that
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
is defined for
\alpha\in(-\frac{4}{\gamma},Q)
,
P\in\mathbb{R}
and is analytic as a function of
q\in\mathbb{C}
in a ball
B_{r}(0)
of radius
r
greater than
\frac{1}{2}
when
\alpha\in[0,Q)
.
We refer the reader to
[
24
, Appendix A]
and
[
23
, Section 1.1]
for a summary of the algebraic approach to conformal blocks on one-point torus.

In previous works
[
36
]
, the semiclassical limit of the
n
-point Liouville correlation function has been shown to be governed by the accessory parameters of a second-order Fuchsian differential equation, with the Heun equation (see, e.g., references) being a notable example. On the other hand, a similar description of the semiclassical limit of Liouville conformal blocks on the torus has been explored in the physics literature, where it is related to the Lamé equation

(1.3)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-m(m-1)\wp(z)+\mathcal{E}\right)\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;q)=0,

where
\wp(z)
is the Weierstrass elliptic function and
\mathcal{E}
is called the
accessory parameter
. This equation arises naturally in the study of Schrödinger equations with periodic potentials and has deep connections to uniformization of Riemann surfaces.

Our objective is to understand the behavior and properties of these Liouville conformal blocks
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
in the semi-classical limit
\gamma\to 0
, shedding light on the underlying probabilistic structures associated with conformal field theories on tori and the possible connection of the conformal block with the Lamé equation. Our main result shows that the semi-classical limit of
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
determines this accessory parameter. Such a proof for all other geometries remains an open question.

A crucial gap lies in establishing a systematic way of defining probabilistic conformal blocks for general Riemann surfaces and prescribed singularities; once this is achieved, our methods may be adapted to explore their semi-classical limits.

Theorem 1.1
.

The semi-classical limit of the conformal block
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
exists and has the form

(1.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)=\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})+\widetilde{f}(\alpha_{0},P_{0});\qquad\alpha=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma},\qquad P=\frac{P_{0}}{\gamma},

for
q=e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau}\in B_{r_{0}}(0)
for some
r_{0}>0
,
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
, and
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Here,
\phi(\cdot)
is an analytic function in
q
given by

\displaystyle\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}+\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\right)\log q-2\alpha_{0}\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}\sum_{n,m\geq 1}\frac{q^{2nm}}{n}-\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{0}^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Phi^{2}]-\alpha_{0}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}

(1.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{q}(x)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right),

where
\Phi
is defined in (
4.217
),
\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx
, and
h_{q}(x):=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)
with
\widetilde{\Psi}
,
h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)
defined in (
4.198
). The function
\widetilde{f}(\cdot)
is a normalization constant such that

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 0}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)=0.

Moreover, the function
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
in (
1.4
) determines the accessory parameter of the Lamé equation

(1.6)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-1\right)\wp(z)+\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}{\phi}(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})\right)\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)=0,

where
\wp(\cdot)
is the Weierstrass
\wp
-function (see (
A.11
)). Through the study of semi-classical conformal blocks (Theorem
4.2
), the function
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
arises as the limit

(1.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)e^{-\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}=\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q),

and is given by

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)

\displaystyle=e^{P_{0}z\pi/2}\theta_{1}(z)^{\alpha_{0}/4}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}z/4}\exp\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(q)M_{\gamma}(z,q)\right]+\frac{1}{2}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}-\alpha_{0}\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(\alpha_{0}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n})n}\left(\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\right)\cos\left(2\pi\left(z-\frac{\tau}{2}\right)n\right)\right)

(1.8)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)-\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}h_{q}(x)\right)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right),

where
\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx
,
\Phi
and
M_{\gamma}(z,q)
are defined in (
4.217
) and (
4.220
) respectively, and
\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)
is the unique solution to the fixed point equation

(1.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)=\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{z,q}(x)-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}K(x,y)\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(y)\,d\mu_{\Psi}(y),

with
K(x,y):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2}{n}(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})\cos(2\pi n(x-y))
and

(1.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}^{(0)}_{z,q}(x):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}q^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{(1-q^{2n})}-\frac{q^{n}(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}{1-q^{2n}}\right)\cos\left(2\pi n\left(z-\frac{\tau}{2}-x\right)\right).

Furthermore, the accessory parameter
\partial_{\tau}\phi(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is related to the Hamiltonian of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model (Definition
4.1
) as follows. Let
u(\tau)
be the solution of the corresponding equations of motion for the Calogero-Moser model (
4.8
) with
m^{2}=(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}
, and let
\tau_{\star}
be a zero of
u(\tau)
, i.e.,
u(\tau_{\star})=0
. Then

(1.11)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{\star}}=-\frac{H(\tau_{\star})}{4},\qquad q_{\star}=e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau_{\star}},

where
H(\tau_{\star})\equiv H(\tau_{\star},\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is the Hamiltonian (
4.9
) evaluated at
\tau=\tau_{\star}
.

Remark 1.1
.

The limit in (
1.4
) can be shown for
q\in B_{r}(0)
for some
r>\frac{1}{2}
when
\alpha_{0}\in[0,2)
. Furthermore, since
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
has the explicit expression (
1.8
), one can use the Lamé equation (
1.6
) to write the explicit form of the accessory parameter
\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
(see Remark
4.4
). The analytic properties with respect to
\alpha_{0}
,
P_{0}
,
q
of the semi-classical limit are accessible through this explicit form.

1.1.
Zamolodchikov conjecture

The main result of this paper rigorously proves the Zamolodchikov conjecture for the case of conformal blocks on a one-point torus. A long standing conjecture formulated by Zamolodchikov in 1986
[
71
, (2.30)-(2.32)]
,
[
38
, Conjectures A, B]
postulates that, in the semi-classical limit (
c\to\infty
), the conformal blocks of a four-point sphere have an exponential structure. Furthermore, it is known that, assuming such an exponential structure, the semi-classical limit of the sphere 4-point conformal block is described by the accessory parameter of the Heun’s equation.

There are three different heuristics that support the exponential structure of the Liouville conformal blocks in the semiclassical regime: the analysis of the power series representation of conformal blocks
[
73
]
, the oscillator formalism of Virasoro algebra
[
10
]
, and the combinatorial expression in terms of the Nekrasov-Okounkov functions describing the partition functions of supersymmetric gauge theories
[
48
]
given by the Alday-Gaiotto-Tachikawa (AGT) correspondence
[
1
]
(see
[
49
,
47
]
for the results on the semi-classical limit).

The semi-classical limit of conformal blocks carries significant importance in mathematical physics as it plays a crucial role in a variety of areas from black hole physics
[
2
,
14
]
and supersymmetric gauge theories
[
47
]
(see also the introduction in
[
53
]
) to Teichmüller theory (see
[
6
,
63
]
and references therein).

Of particular interest to this paper is the connection between semi-classical conformal blocks and the Heun equation. The Heun equation is a second-order differential equation that describes isomonodromic deformations on a Riemann sphere with four simple poles and features a free parameter known as the
accessory parameter
. This parameter is crucial in the semi-classical analysis as it is characterized by conformal blocks on the sphere
[
71
]
. The study of the Heun equation is interesting on its own, and has deep ties to black hole physics and hyperbolic geometry among other applications. See
[
61
]
for a bibliographical account and
[
30
]
for a review on applications to physics.

In the recent years, interest has grown in understanding the relationships between conformal blocks, accessory parameters, and Painlevé equations
[
9
,
38
,
55
,
37
]
. Specifically, the semi-classical limit of conformal blocks is related to the Painlevé VI equation; the semi-classical block on a four-point sphere turns out to be the solution of certain Hamilton-Jacobi equation associated to the Painlevé VI equation
[
40
,
34
]
. In turn the Hamiltonian of the Painlevé VI equation is given by
c=1
conformal blocks on sphere, and hence, the aforementioned relation provides a non-trivial link between the
c\to\infty
and
c=1
conformal blocks.

More recently, a relation between
c\to\infty
conformal blocks and the connection problem for the degenerations of the Heun equation was conjectured in
[
12
]
and is dubbed ’the Trieste formula’
[
39
]
, with the case of the Heun equation (without degenerations) verified in
[
39
]
.
So, the proof of Zamolodchikov conjecture paves the way for a rigorous treatment of the aforementioned problems. Indeed most of the above claims could be easily adapted to the one-point torus case. In particular, the role of the Heun equation is now played by the Lamé equation and furthermore, its accessory parameter, under the assumption that the Zamolodchikov conjecture holds, is known to be related to the semi-classical conformal block on the torus
[
54
]
and to the elliptic form of the Painlevé VI equation with special value of the parameters
[
9
]
. In this context, we also want to highlight a similar study of the semi-classical limit of Liouville CFT on a sphere
[
36
]
, where they prove the Takhtajan-Zograf theorem relating the classical Liouville action at special points and accessory parameters of a second order Fuchsian equation of holomorphic functions on the unit disk
[
64
]
.

In Theorem
1.1
, we showed that, as the central charge
c
goes to
\infty
or equivalently
\gamma\to 0
, the torus one point Liouville conformal block behaves as
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)\sim\exp(\phi(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)/\gamma^{2})
where
\phi(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is the semi-classical conformal block. Moreover, (
1.6
) showed that
\phi(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is related to the accessory parameter of Lamé equation. As a result, we provide a rigorous proof of the analogue of Zamolodchikov conjecture for one-point torus conformal blocks. Consequently, we prove that
\phi(\ldots)
is also related to the Hamiltonian of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser (NAECM) model (see Theorem
4.2
) for special values of the solution
[
9
]
, which in turn is related to
c=1
conformal blocks on a one-point torus
[
17
]
. Such a proof for all other geometries remains an open question.

1.2.
Ideas of the proofs

Our main goal in this paper is to prove the existence and estimate the radius of convergence for the semi-classical limit (
\gamma\to 0
) of the conformal block
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)
. Henceforth we refer to this as the
undeformed
conformal block. We will soon introduce a
deformed
conformal block which, roughly speaking, will involve a shift of the variable
x\to x+z
. The proof of the statement of existence involves several steps. One of the main challenges is to show that the conformal block has exponential structure in the semi-classical limit, i.e., as
\gamma\to 0
, the torus 1-point conformal block
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
behaves as
\exp\big(-\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}(\phi(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)+\tilde{f}(\alpha_{0},P_{0}))\big)
. For showing this, we use the probabilistic description of
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
given as

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}\sim\mathbb{E}\left[\left({\sf IGMC}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right],\qquad{\sf IGMC}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q):=\int^{1}_{0}\theta_{1}(x)^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}dx

where
:Y(x;q):\,\,=Y(x;q)-\frac{\gamma}{4}\mathbb{E}\left[Y(x;q)^{2}\right]
is a renormalized log-correlated field on
[0,1]
(Wick notation), and
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}dx
is the Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos (GMC) measure (see also Definition
2.1
).

The above representation of the conformal block was introduced by
[
24
]
and expressed in terms of the Gaussian multiplicative chaos measure constructed using the log-correlated field
Y(x;q)
defined for
x\in[0,1]
. See Definition
2.2
for further discussion. With the choice of scaling
P=\frac{P_{0}}{\gamma}
and
\alpha=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}
for
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the first difficulty is to show that
\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
does not blow up to infinity. We overcome this difficulty through a combination of Hölder’s inequality, Selberg integral asymptotics, and explicit negative moment bounds for GMC integrals.

We first show that proving the boundedness of the conformal block is equivalent to proving the boundedness of the expression on the LHS of the expression below. For the upper bound when
\alpha_{0}\in[0,2)
, we use a comparison argument: since the covariance
\mathbb{E}[Y(x;q)Y(y;q)]\geq\mathbb{E}[Y(x)Y(y)]-2\log(\prod_{k\geq 1}(1+q^{k})^{2})
, Slepian’s inequality reduces the problem to bounding negative moments of the
q
-independent GMC integral
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}:=\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx
. These negative moments are controlled using Lemma
3.2
, which provides bounds via the exact formulae of the negative moments of GMC integral
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
from
[
3
]
. For
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,0)
, we instead bound positive moments using Selberg integral asymptotics: the
N
-th moment of the GMC integral can be written as

(1.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{N}\right]=\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{i<j}|\sin(\pi(x_{i}-x_{j}))|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\sin(\pi x_{i}))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}dx_{i},

which has an explicit evaluation in terms of Gamma functions. Taking
N\sim|\alpha_{0}|/\gamma^{2}
and applying Stirling’s approximation shows that these moments grow at most as
\exp(C/\gamma^{2})
. These bounds establish that the expression
\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
remains bounded as
\gamma\to 0
, proving the tightness of the sequence.

Once the tightness is established, it remains to show that all subsequential limits of
\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
are the same. To this end, we use the deformation of the conformal block
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
which we denote as
\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,\tau)
(see Definition
2.4
) for
\chi\in\{\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{2}{\gamma}\}
,
z\in(0,1)
, and
\alpha,P,\tau
are defined as before. The deformed conformal block on the torus is uniquely determined as the solution of a differential equation named the Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov (BPZ) equation,

\Big(\partial_{z}^{2}-l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)\wp(z)+2\mathbf{i}\pi\chi^{2}\partial_{\tau}\Big)\psi_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,\tau)=0,\qquad\ell_{\chi}=\frac{\chi^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha\chi}{2}

which is defined on the punctured torus.

The key technical innovation in our approach is the use of Girsanov’s theorem (Cameron-Martin formula) to perform a detailed asymptotic expansion for
\gamma\to 0
of the deformed conformal block. Through the Girsanov representation, we factor the
z
-dependence from the
\gamma
-dependence, introducing auxiliary Gaussian variables that couple the
q
-dependent terms with the GMC integral. Specifically, we introduce a tilted Gaussian variable
\Psi
and apply the Cameron-Martin theorem:

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\Psi}f(Y)\right]=e^{\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{Var}(\Psi)}\mathbb{E}\left[f(Y+h_{\Psi})\right],

where
h_{\Psi}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\Psi\cdot Y(x)]
represents the Cameron-Martin shift. This transformation allows us to extract the leading-order
O(\gamma^{-2})
terms explicitly while controlling the remainder through Proposition
3.1
, which establishes the convergence of the shifted GMC expectation to a quadratic chaos expression.

The convergence result in Proposition
3.1
, established in Section
3
, is central to completing the asymptotic analysis. This proposition shows that the GMC integral
(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}:d\mu)^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}
, when appropriately renormalized, converges to an expression involving the quadratic Wick-ordered field
:Y(x)^{2}:
. Our approach is similar in spirit to that of
[
36
]
, but with a key technical differences. Rather than white noise decomposition, we work directly with the spectral (Fourier) structure of the log-correlated field
Y(x)
, employing the explicit negative moment formula from
[
3
]
and Abel summation techniques to control spectral tails. These modifications are naturally suited to the conformal block setting where the field
Y(x)
has an intrinsic Fourier decomposition.

On the one hand, for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
, we show in Theorem
4.2
that the deformed conformal block
\psi_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,\tau)
factorizes as
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)\times e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}
as
\gamma\to 0
. The function
\widetilde{\Gamma}
emerges from a refined asymptotic expansion where the Cameron-Martin shift in this case is denoted by
h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)
and has the expansion
h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)=h_{q}(x)/\gamma+\gamma\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)+O(\gamma^{2})
, where the function
\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)
satisfies a fixed point equation arising from the
O(\gamma)
correction. Similar decomposition of deformed conformal block is a direct consequence of the value of
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
which is called light insertion in the literature, and was previously observed in
[
40
,
54
]
for the case of the torus. With the scaling
\alpha=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}
and
P=\frac{P_{0}}{\gamma}
, substituting the expression
\psi_{\gamma/2,P}^{\alpha}(z,\tau)\sim\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}
into the BPZ equation above and studying the limit
\gamma\to 0
gives us the Lamé equation as shown in (
1.6
). Since
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is explicit (see (
4.161
)), the accessory parameter of (
1.6
) which could be the
\tau
-derivative of any subsequential limit of
\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
is now uniquely identified, which in turn shows the existence of the limit of the sequence
\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
.

On the other hand, for
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
, following a similar strategy as above, the BPZ equation in the
\gamma\to 0
limit in this case becomes the Hamilton-Jacobi equation for the (non-autonomous) elliptic Calogero-Moser model (NAECM). The asymptotic expansion via the Cameron-Martin approach yields an explicit expression for
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)

plus contributions from the Cameron-Martin shift
h_{\Psi}
, and
\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]
for a Gaussian variable
\Psi
.
The tightness in
C^{2}([M,1-M]\times[\epsilon,q_{0}])
for
0<M<1/2
,
0<\epsilon<q_{0}\in(0,1)
established in Proposition
4.2
, combined with the commutativity of limits
\gamma\to 0
and
q\to q_{0}
, allows us to demonstrate in Theorem
4.1
that the semi-classical limit of
\psi^{\alpha}_{2/\gamma,P}
solves the aforementioned Hamilton-Jacobi equation. See Definition
4.1
for a discussion. This connection between the deformed conformal block and Calogero-Moser model is used later in Theorem
4.2
to show that the accessory parameter of the Lamé equation (
1.6
) is in fact related to the Hamiltonian of the NAECM model computed at some special points, a statement that is known in the literature. See
[
9
]
and references therein.

1.3.
Outline of the paper

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we review the probabilistic construction of the
undeformed
conformal block
\,\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)
, and the
deformed
conformal block which satisfies the PDE known as the Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov (BPZ) equation.

In Section
3
, we establish the key analytical result underlying the semi-classical analysis: the convergence of GMC integrals raised to large negative powers toward quadratic chaos expressions. The main result is Proposition
3.1
, which shows that for a probability measure
\mu
with density bounded below, the appropriately renormalized GMC integral converges as
\gamma\to 0
to a finite expectation involving the Wick-ordered quadratic field
:Y(x)^{2}:
. The proof relies on three supporting lemmas: Lemma
3.1
establishes uniform integrability using spectral truncation and Janson’s hypercontractive estimates, Lemma
3.2
provides negative moment bounds via the explicit formula from
[
3
]
, and Lemma
3.3
controls the approximation error between truncated and full GMC integrals using Girsanov arguments with Abel summation techniques.

In Section
4
, we formulate the semi-classical limit of the
deformed

conformal block. In Subsection
4.1
we study the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block for
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
. The main result of this Subsection is Theorem
4.1
which establishes an explicit description of such a semi-classical limit. In Subsection
4.2
we analyse the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
. The main result of this Subsection is Theorem
4.2
which gives explicit description of that semi-classical limit in terms of the solution of Lamé equation. As a consequence of this theorem, we obtain explicit expressions for the solution and accessory parameter of the Lamé equation.

We prove Theorem
1.1
in Section
5
. We further prove that the radius of convergence of the semi-classical conformal block is positive in Theorem
5.2
.

Finally, in Section
6
, we study the asymptotic behaviour of the semi-classical limit of the conformal blocks for
\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty
. The key component of the analysis is the expression of the normalization constant of the undeformed conformal block in terms of double Gamma functions. Results in this section are used to prove results in Section
4
.

1.4.
Outlook

Our paper presents two key takeaways. Firstly, the methods we introduced in proving the Zamolodchikov conjecture offer a road map for studying semi-classical conformal blocks on other Riemann surfaces and for the analysis of conformal blocks with different central charges conditional on the probabilistic formulation of conformal blocks. Secondly, the explicit formulas for the conformal blocks and the Lamé equation obtained here provide profound insights into the analytic and geometric aspects of conformal field theories (CFTs) and the associated Heun-type equations. Let us now describe some open questions of immediate interest.

•

A natural question that arises is the generalization of our techniques to other geometries such as the four-point sphere and higher genus surfaces. The key to achieving this would be to develop a systematic approach to defining probabilistic conformal blocks on Riemann surfaces. The formulation of probabilistic conformal blocks on a four point sphere is expected to have a slightly varied construction
[
23
]
, and the irregular conformal blocks can then be constructed through appropriate degeneration limits. The proof of the Zamolodchikov conjecture in the aforementioned cases is expected to follow a similar formalism to that presented here.

•

Semi-classical limit of Liouville CFT corresponds to taking the central charge
c\to\infty
. It is natural to ask what happens when
c\to-\infty
. This question is related to the semi-classical limit of CFTs with
c\leq 1
(i.e.,
\gamma
purely imaginary). Recently, in a series of works
[
68
,
66
,
67
]
, Yilin Wang has investigated the large deviation problem of Schramm-Loewner evolution (
\mathrm{SLE}_{\kappa}
) as
\kappa\to 0_{+}
and has shown that the large deviation rate function is equal to the Loewner energy function. Since
\kappa\to 0_{+}
corresponds to talking
c\to-\infty
and SLE is related to the correlation function of certain CFT, the results of
[
68
,
66
,
67
]
describes semi-classical limit for
c\to-\infty
. In a more recent work
[
52
]
, Peltola and Wang characterized minimizers of the Loewner potential using tools from real algebraic geometry which provides unique insight about the CFT when the central charges diminishes to
-\infty
. Moreover, the minimum value of such Loewener energy is shown to solve a PDE which looks similar to Hamilton-Jacobi limit type equation satisfied by the semi-classical limit of deformed conformal block with degenerate weight of
\frac{2}{\gamma}
in (
4.7
) of Theorem
4.1
.

•

The Trieste formula
[
12
, (4.1.16) - (4.1.17)]
,
[
39
, eq. 2.20b]
describes the connection formulae of the Heun’s equation in terms of the semi-classical limit of the fusion kernel of the 4-point sphere conformal blocks.
Our description of the semi-classical conformal block may enable the proof of an analogue to the Trieste formula connecting the semi-classical limit of the modular kernel (see
[
23
, (5.3)]
) of one-point torus conformal block with the connection formulae of the Lamé equation.
A probabilistic description of the four-point conformal block and its degenerations could prove the Trieste formula in full generality.

•

In
[
38
]
, the authors found a series representation of the accessory parameter of the Heun’s equation where the coefficients satisfy some recurrence relation. This is reminiscent of the Zamolodchkiov recurrence relation satisfied by the coefficients of the conformal blocks. Following a similar procedure as in
[
38
]
, one can also derive a series representation of the accessory parameter of Lamé equation once the monodromy data associated to
\alpha_{0},P_{0}
is determined. Furthermore, the accessory parameter is also explicitly given in terms of the Cameron-Martin shift and known elliptic functions in (
4.157
), and in terms of integrals (
4.287
). Such formulae would allow to study its asymptotic properties
[
51
,
69
]
.

•

Through the AGT correspondence, the semi-classical conformal block on a one-point torus determines the Nekrasov-Shatashvili (NS) limit of the corresponding
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
gauge theory, or equivalently, the effective twisted superpotential
[
65
]
. The explicit form
of the classical conformal block in terms of the Cameron-Martin shift and known elliptic function (see Theorem
5.1
) presented in this paper would enable a rigorous proof of the Nekrasov-Rosly-Shatashvili conjecture that relates the effective twisted superpotential of the gauge theory to the so-called Yang-Yang function of the associated quantum Hitchin system
[
46
,
45
,
29
]
.

•

The conformal blocks for arbitrary central charge solve certain bilinear relations called the Nakajima-Yoshioka blow-up relations
[
43
]
. Such relations for the one-point torus have been written in
[
25
,
9
]
and it will be interesting to see if such relations could be proved using probabilistic conformal blocks. An interesting property of the blow-up relation is that it connects the semi-classical conformal blocks with the conformal block at central charge
c=1

[
47
]
, in turn relating Painlevé equations to Heun equations for the sphere case and the NAECM model to Lamé equation (see also
[
9
]
) for the torus case. We show the latter relation in Theorem
4.2
based on probabilistic ideas. Showing the full blow-up relation is beyond the scope of this paper and we leave this for a future work.

2.
Probabilistic conformal blocks

In this section we recall the construction of the conformal block in
[
24
]
. We begin by defining Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos (GMC) measures in terms of log-correlated fields, which will then be used to define the
probabilistic
one point conformal block on the torus. We then introduce the notion of a
deformed
conformal block which solves the PDE known as the Belavin-Polyakov-Zamolodchikov (BPZ) equation on the torus with one puncture.

2.1.
Log-correlated fields and Gaussian Multiplicative Chaos (GMC) measures

For
x\in[0,1]
, the random fields
Y_{N}(x;q)
,
Y_{N}(x)
, with
N\in\mathbb{N}
are defined as

(2.1)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\Big(a_{n}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n}\sin(2\pi nx)\Big),

(2.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x)+\underbrace{2\sum_{n,m=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{nm}}{\sqrt{n}}\Big(a_{n,m}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n,m}\sin(2\pi nx)\Big)}_{=:F(x;q)},

where
\{a_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
,
\{b_{n}\}_{n\geq 1}
,
\{a_{n,m}\}_{n,m\geq 1}
,
\{b_{n,m}\}_{n,m\geq 1}
are sequences of i.i.d standard real Gaussian random variables, the modular parameter
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
, and
q=e^{i\pi\tau}
.
In what follows we use the notation
1
1

1

Throughout this paper, we also use the dependence on
q
and
\tau
interchangeably.

(2.3)

\displaystyle Y(x):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x),

\displaystyle Y(x;q):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q).

Indeed, the above limit exists. The first limit exist in the sense of weak convergence in the Sobolev space
H^{-s}([0,1])
and the second limit exists to due to the first limit and the almost sure convergence of infinite series of
F(x;q)
due to the term
q^{nm}
.
The resulting random fields
Y(\cdot;q)
and
Y(\cdot)
are log-correlated, and
the covariance kernels of (
2.3
) for
\tau\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
are given as follows.

•

The field
Y(x)
is log-correlated on
[0,1]
:

(2.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y(x)Y(y)]=-2\log|e^{2\mathbf{i}\pi x}-e^{2\mathbf{i}\pi y}|,

which is the usual expression
\log|x-y|
in cylindrical coordinates obtained by the transformation
z\rightarrow e^{2\pi iz}
.

•

The
q
-dependent field
Y(x;q)
is log correlated on the torus
\mathbb{C}/(\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z})
:

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y(x;q)Y(y;q)]=-2\log|\theta_{1}(x-y)|+2\log|q^{1/6}\eta(q)|.

Note that for
q\rightarrow 0
and
(x-y)\to 0
, the above relations (
2.4
), (
2.5
) coincide up to a trivial factor. The eta function
\eta(q)
is defined in (
A.6
), the theta function
\theta_{1}(z)
is defined in (
A.3
), and
\theta_{1}(0)=0
.

Using
[
24
, Lemma 2.5]
, one finds that

\mathbb{E}\left[F(x;q)^{2}\right]=-4\log|q^{-1/12}\eta(q)|.

As a result, we get

(2.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q)^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x)^{2}\right]-4\log|q^{-1/12}\eta(q)|.

With the above log-correlated fields, we now define the following random measures.

Definition 2.1

(GMC measure)
.

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
and
\tau\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the GMC
measures
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx
and
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx
are defined to be the weak limits of
measures in probability with respect to the filtration generated by
\{({a}_{n},{b}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1-}

(2.7)

\begin{split}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx&:=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x)-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x)^{2}]}dx,\\
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx&:=\lim_{N\to\infty}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q)-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q)^{2}]}dx.\end{split}

Since we are defining
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y}
instead of
e^{\gamma Y}
, for
\gamma\in(0,2)
, the corresponding Gaussian multiplicative chaos measure is indeed in
L^{2}
phase. Hence the usual martingale argument for showing the existence and uniqueness of the limiting random measures suffices.

For a test function
f(x)
, these measures are interpreted as

(2.8)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}f(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}f(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q)-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{Y}_{N}(x;q)^{2}]}dx,

and a similar expression holds for the
q
-independent field
Y(x)
.

The existence of the limits above can be shown in the following way. Note that the identity

(2.9)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{c\left(\widetilde{Y}_{k}-\widetilde{Y}_{k-1}\right)}]=e^{\frac{c^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[(\widetilde{Y}_{k}-\widetilde{Y}_{k-1})^{2}]},

implies that the sequence in (
2.8
) is a martingale with respect to the filtration generated by the sequence of random variables
\{({a}_{n},{b}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
. Since the sequences is almost surely positive, an almost sure convergence is guaranteed by the martingale convergence theorem, which in turn proves the existence of the limit in (
2.8
).
More details about the existence of the limits could be found in
[
59
,
8
]
.

2.2.
One-point (undeformed) conformal block

We now recall the definition of the one-point conformal block in
[
24
]
.

Definition 2.2
.

For
\alpha\in(-\frac{4}{\gamma},Q)
,
and
P\in\mathbb{R}
,
q=e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau}
,
\tau\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, or equivalently
q\in(0,1)
, we define the
probabilistic
1
-point toric conformal block as

(2.10)

\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q):=\frac{1}{Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right],

with the normalization

(2.11)

Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q):=q^{\frac{1}{12}(\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}-1)}\eta(q)^{\alpha^{2}+1-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}(2{\sin(\pi x)})^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}{e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right].

The theta function
\theta_{1}(x)
is defined in (
A.3
), and has the behavior
-2q^{1/4}\sin(\pi x)
near
x=0
. Further definitions of elliptic functions are provided in
A
.

Note that the normalization constant
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
ensures that

\displaystyle\lim_{q\rightarrow 0}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=1,

\displaystyle\lim_{P\rightarrow\infty}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=q^{\frac{1}{12}}\eta(q)^{-1}.

Definition 2.3
.

The analytic extension of the conformal block in Definition
2.2
indeed also has a probabilistic expression, thanks to Cameron-Martin theorem as stated and proved in Lemma 2.12 of
[
24
]
. We write below such probabilistic formula of analytic extension:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=\frac{1}{Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}}(q^{1/6}\eta(q))^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}e^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}(2{\sin(\pi x)})^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}{e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]

where
F(x;q)
is a smooth Gaussian field on
[0,1]
defined in (
2.2
) and

(2.13)

\mathcal{Q}(q):=\exp\left[\sqrt{2}\sum_{m,n=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}\left(a_{n,m}a_{n}+b_{n,m}b_{n}\right)-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}a_{m,n}\right)^{2}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}b_{m,n}\right)^{2}\right]

A key consequence of the present construction is that the analytic properties of the conformal block (
2.10
) become accessible, and we recall the following result.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
24
, Theorem 1.1]
)
.

For
\gamma\in(0,2)
,
\alpha\in(-\frac{4}{\gamma},Q)
, and
P\in\mathbb{R}
, the conformal block in (
2.10
) is analytic in a complex neighborhood of
q=0
. Moreover, for
\alpha\in[0,Q)
, the analytic extension of (
2.10
) exists in a neighborhood of
q=0
with to
|q|<r_{0}
for some
r_{0}>1/2
.
Q
is defined in (
1.2
).

Remark 2.1
.

The range
(-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}},Q)
for
\alpha
is the range in which the 1-point correlation function
\langle e^{\alpha\phi(0)}\rangle_{\tau}
has a GMC expression from the path integral formalism of LCFT
[
16
]
. Furthermore, the range
\alpha\in(0,Q)
is needed to extend the probabilistic definition of conformal block in disk of radius greater than
\frac{1}{2}
.

Furthermore, (
2.10
) can be represented a series with the coefficients satisfying the so-called Zamolodchikov recursion
[
24
, Proposition 2.11]
, and as a combinatorial series in terms of Nekrasov-Okounkov functions
[
24
, Section 2.3]
due to the Alday-Gaiotto-Tachikawa (AGT) correspondence
[
44
]
.

Remark 2.2
.

For
-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}=N<\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}
with
N\in\mathbb{N}
, (
2.10
) can be expressed as a Dotsenko-Fateev integral
[
42
,
22
]

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\gamma\pi Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C}\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\theta_{1}(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}|\theta_{1}(x_{i})|^{\frac{N\gamma^{2}}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}dx_{i},

with

(2.14)

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{\frac{N\gamma P\pi}{2}}}{\left(\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\right)\right)^{N}}\left(\frac{|\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)|}{2}\right)^{\frac{N(N+1)\gamma^{2}}{8}}\prod_{k=1}^{N}\frac{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{k\gamma^{2}}{8}\right)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{(2N+1-k)\gamma^{2}}{8}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1+\frac{k\gamma^{2}}{8}+\frac{\mathbf{i}\gamma P}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(1+\frac{k\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\mathbf{i}\gamma P}{2}\right)}.

Let us highlight the following result that will prove useful to study the
q\to 0
asymptotics of the semi-classical conformal blocks.

Proposition 2.1
.

The partition function
Z_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}
in (
2.11
) can be expressed in terms of double Gamma functions as follows
[
24
, (2.15), Proposition 6.4]

(2.15)

\displaystyle Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}=q^{\frac{1}{12}(\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}-1)}\eta(q)^{\alpha^{2}+1-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}\mathcal{A}_{\gamma,P}(\alpha),

where the
q
-independent factor is

(2.16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\gamma,P}(\alpha)=e^{\frac{i\pi\alpha^{2}}{2}}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}\right)^{\frac{\gamma\alpha}{4}}e^{-\frac{\pi\alpha P}{2}}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}

(2.17)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2}-\mathbf{i}P\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2}+\mathbf{i}P\right)}{\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{2}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(Q-\mathbf{i}P\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(Q+\mathbf{i}P\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(Q-\alpha\right)},

where the function
\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\cdot)
is the ratio of double Gamma functions

\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(x)=\frac{\Gamma_{2}(x|\gamma,\gamma^{-1})}{\Gamma_{2}\left({Q}/{2}|\gamma,\gamma^{-1}\right)},

and
Q
is defined in (
1.2
).

2.3.
z
-deformed conformal block

We now define the probabilistic equivalent of inserting a degenerate field
2
2

2

To be precise, the
z
-deformed conformal block is a two-point conformal block on the torus with one degenerate insertion.

\mathcal{V}
inside conformal block as

\mathcal{V}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q):=\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{1}(z+x)^{\frac{\gamma\chi}{2}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{\gamma\chi}{2}}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx\qquad\textrm{for }z\in\mathfrak{B},\quad\chi\in\left\{\frac{2}{\gamma},\frac{\gamma}{2}\right\}

with
\mathfrak{B}:=\{z:0<\mathrm{Im}(z)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}
. The deformed conformal block is then defined in terms of
\mathcal{V}
as follows.

Definition 2.4
.

For
z\in(0,1)
,
q\in(0,1)
,
\alpha\in\left(-\frac{4}{\gamma}+\chi,Q\right)
,
P\in\mathbb{R}
, the two point toric conformal block with one degenerate insertion, known as the
z
-deformed probabilistic conformal block is defined as

(2.18)

\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,\tau):=\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{V}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right],

where

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(q):=q^{\frac{P^{2}}{2}+\frac{\gamma l_{\chi}}{12\chi}-\frac{1}{6}\frac{l_{\chi}^{2}}{\chi^{2}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)^{-\frac{2l_{\chi}^{2}}{3\chi^{2}}+\frac{l_{\chi}}{3}+\frac{4l_{\chi}}{3\gamma\chi}},

\displaystyle l_{\chi}=\frac{\chi^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha\chi}{2},

and
\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)
given by (
A.6
).

Theorem 2.2

(
[
24
, Proposition 3.2]
)
.

The deformed conformal block (
2.18
) is convergent in the domain
D^{\alpha}_{\chi}
where

(2.20)

D^{\alpha}_{\chi}:=\{(q,z):|q|<r_{\alpha-\chi}\textrm{ and }z\in{\mathfrak{B}}\},\qquad{\mathfrak{B}}:=\{z:0<\mathrm{Im}(z)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}

Here
r_{\alpha}>0
for
\alpha\in(-\frac{4}{\gamma},Q)
.

Remark 2.3
.

1.

The choice of the pre-factor
\mathcal{W}(q)
in (
2.18
) is dictated by the BPZ equation which will be satisfied by
\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,\tau)
(see Theorem
2.3
).

2.

The deformed and the undeformed conformal blocks are related as

(2.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{z\to 0}\left(\theta_{1}(z)^{l_{\chi}}\psi_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)\right)=\mathcal{W}(q)Z^{\alpha-\chi}_{\gamma,P}(q)\,\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha-\chi}(q),

\displaystyle q=e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau}.

We now state the BPZ equation for
\psi_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,\tau)
.

Theorem 2.3

(
[
24
, Theorem 3.5]
)
.

The
z
-deformed toric conformal block satisfies the following BPZ equation

(2.22)

\Big(\partial_{z}^{2}-l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)\wp(z)+2\mathbf{i}\pi\chi^{2}\partial_{\tau}\Big)\psi_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,\tau)=0\qquad\textrm{for }(q,z)\in D^{\alpha}_{\chi},

where
\alpha\in(-\frac{4}{\gamma}+\chi,Q)
,
\chi\in\{\frac{\gamma}{2},\frac{2}{\gamma}\}
, and
\wp(z)
is Weierstrass
\wp
-function.

3.
Semi-classical limit of GMC integrals

In this section, we establish the key analytical result that underlies the semi-classical analysis of conformal blocks: the convergence of GMC integrals raised to large negative powers toward quadratic chaos expressions. The main result is Proposition
3.1
, which shows that for a probability measure
\mu
on
[0,1]
with density bounded below,

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)\right]

exists and equals a finite expectation involving the quadratic
Wick-ordered field
:Y(x)^{2}:
.

We highlight here the similarities between our approach and that in
[
36
]
. Our Proposition
3.1
is similar in spirit to Proposition 4.2 of
[
36
]
, and the overall proof strategy follows a comparable structure. In particular, as in
[
36
]
, we show that the quantity
(\int^{1}_{0}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}d\mu(x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}
, when renormalized by
e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\int^{1}_{0}Y(x)d\mu(x)}
, converges almost surely to a limiting expression involving the quadratic Wick-ordered field. This almost sure convergence is a crucial ingredient in proving the semi-classical limits established in Sections
4
and
5
.

While the overall proof architecture of Proposition
3.1
parallels that of
[
36
]
, several of the technical estimates require different arguments tailored to the specific structure of the log-correlated field
Y(x)
and the GMC measure constructed from it. Most notably, the negative moment estimate in Lemma
3.2
employs the explicit moment formula from
[
3
]
rather than the Fyodorov-Bouchaud formula
[
36
]
. Similarly, the approximation error bounds in Lemma
3.3
are established by controlling tail probabilities finite truncation of error of the Gaussian multiplicative chaos measure of the log-correlated field
Y(\cdot)
with Abel summation techniques for the spectral tail, rather than white noise decomposition methods.
These modifications are necessitated by the spectral (Fourier) structure of the field
Y(x;q)
that arises naturally in the conformal block setting.

This proposition plays a central role in the proofs of the main theorems of this paper:

•

In Theorem
5.1
, Proposition
3.1
is applied after the Cameron-Martin transformation to establish that the final expectation in the asymptotic expansion (
5.19
) converges as
\gamma\to 0
, thereby completing the proof of existence of the semi-classical limit.

•

In Theorem
4.1
, the same proposition ensures that the semi-classical limit of the heavy deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha}_{2/\gamma,P}(z,q)
is well-defined, which is essential for deriving the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
4.7
).

•

In Theorem
4.2
, the proposition is used to identify the explicit form of the function
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
appearing in the Lamé equation (
4.158
).

The proof of Proposition
3.1
relies on three supporting lemmas. Lemma
3.1
establishes uniform integrability of the quadratic chaos using spectral truncation and Janson’s hypercontractive estimates. Lemma
3.2
provides bounds on negative moments of GMC integrals via the Fyodorov-Bouchaud formula. Lemma
3.3
controls the approximation error between truncated and full GMC integrals using Girsanov arguments. Together, these lemmas allow us to pass to the limit
\gamma\to 0
via dominated convergence.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
[0,1]
with density
\rho
satisfying
\rho(x)=(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\alpha_{0}/2}e^{\pi P_{0}x}f(x)
for some continuous function such that
\inf_{x\in[0,1]}f(x)>0
. For every
\alpha_{0}>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):\,d\mu(x)\right)\right]

(3.1)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right].

Moreover, the convergence is uniform in the sense that

(3.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma\in(0,1]}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)-\gamma\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)\right)\right]<\infty.

The proof of Proposition
3.1
relies on three key lemmas, which we now state and prove.

Lemma 3.1

(Uniform integrability of quadratic chaos)
.

For every
\alpha_{0}>0
,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y_{t}(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)-\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y_{t}(x):\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right)\right]<\infty,

where
Y_{t}(x)=\sum_{1\leq n\leq t}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}(a_{n}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n}\sin(2\pi nx))
is the spectral truncation of
Y
.

Proof.

Define
N_{t}:=\int_{0}^{1}:Y_{t}(x):\,d\mu(x)
and
Z_{t}(x):=Y_{t}(x)-N_{t}
. Then we have

(3.4)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}:Y_{t}(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)-N_{t}^{2}=\int_{0}^{1}:Z_{t}(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x).

Fix a large constant
t_{0}>0
. For
t>t_{0}
, we write
Z_{t}=Z_{t_{0}}+\Delta_{t_{0},t}
, where

(3.5)

\displaystyle\Delta_{t_{0},t}(x):=Z_{t}(x)-Z_{t_{0}}(x)=\sum_{t_{0}<n\leq t}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}(a_{n}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n}\sin(2\pi nx)).

Using the inequality
xyz\leq(x^{3}+y^{3}+z^{3})/3
,
we obtain

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\alpha_{0}\int_{0}^{1}:Z_{t}^{2}:\,d\mu}\right]\leq\frac{1}{3}(A_{1}+A_{2}+A_{3}),

where

(3.7)

\displaystyle A_{1}:=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-3\alpha_{0}\int_{0}^{1}:Z_{t_{0}}^{2}:\,d\mu}\right],\qquad A_{2}:=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-3\alpha_{0}\int_{0}^{1}:\Delta_{t_{0},t}^{2}:\,d\mu}\right],\qquad A_{3}:=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-6\alpha_{0}\int_{0}^{1}Z_{t_{0}}\Delta_{t_{0},t}\,d\mu}\right].

Since
Z_{t_{0}}
is a finite-dimensional Gaussian chaos (depending only on finitely many Gaussian random variables), we have
:Z_{t_{0}}(x)^{2}:\geq-C
for some constant
C>0
. Therefore,
A_{1}<\infty
. On the other hand, the random variables
Z_{1}:=\int_{0}^{1}:\Delta_{t_{0},t}^{2}:\,d\mu
and
Z_{2}:=\int_{0}^{1}Z_{t_{0}}\Delta_{t_{0},t}\,d\mu
belong to the second Wiener chaos. By Janson’s hypercontractive tail estimate
[
33
, Theorem 6.7]
, for random variables in the second Wiener chaos,

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}\geq s\|Z_{i}\|_{2})\leq e^{-cs}\quad\text{for }s\geq 2,

where
c>0
is a universal constant. The key observation is that

(3.9)

\displaystyle\text{Cov}(\Delta_{t_{0},t}(x),\Delta_{t_{0},t}(y))=\sum_{n>t_{0}}\frac{2}{n}\cos(2\pi n(x-y))\to 0

uniformly as
t_{0}\to\infty
. This implies that
\|Z_{1}\|_{2}
and
\|Z_{2}\|_{2}
can be made arbitrarily small by choosing
t_{0}
sufficiently large. Therefore, by choosing
t_{0}
large enough, we can ensure that
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
are bounded uniformly in
t>t_{0}
. Combined with the bound on
A_{1}
, this completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 3.2

(Negative moments of the GMC integral)
.

For all
\beta\geq 1
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
,
0<\delta\ll 1
,

(3.10)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right),

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
\beta
and
\gamma
. Consequently,

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\leq\gamma^{\delta/16}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{|\log\gamma|^{2}}{4C\gamma^{2}}\right).

Proof.

Since the density
\rho
of
\mu
is bounded below, we have

(3.12)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\asymp\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\alpha_{0}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:dx.

For the one-dimensional circular log-correlated Gaussian field, the moment formula following identity
[
3
]

(3.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}(\sin\pi x)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi x(P_{0}-\mathbf{i})}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}{Y(x)}}dx\right)^{\frac{-\beta}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

(3.14)

\displaystyle=e^{2\pi P_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{i}P_{0}}{\gamma}-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(-\frac{\mathbf{i}P_{0}}{\gamma}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\left(\frac{-\beta}{2\gamma}\right)}\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{1-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{i}P_{0}}{\gamma}-\frac{\beta}{2\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(-\frac{\mathbf{i}P_{0}}{\gamma}+\frac{\beta}{2\gamma}\right)}.

Using the asymptotics of the double gamma function
\Gamma_{\gamma/2}
as in (
6.1
), we obtain

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{C\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)

for some constant
C>0
. The tail bound (
3.11
) follows from Markov’s inequality, i.e., for
Z=\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\alpha_{0}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu
,

(3.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(Z\leq\gamma)\leq\gamma^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{C\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right).

Optimizing over
\beta
(choosing
\beta=|\log\gamma|/(2C)
) yields the stated bound.
∎

Lemma 3.3

(Approximation error)
.

For any
0<\delta,\epsilon\ll 1
, let
t_{\gamma}:=\gamma^{-1-\epsilon}
. There exist constants
c,C>0
(depending on
\delta,\epsilon
) such that for all sufficiently small
\gamma>0
:

(3.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\left|\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:\right)d\mu(x)\right|\geq\gamma^{\delta/8}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c}{\gamma^{3-\delta/4}}\right).

Proof.

Write
\bar{Y}_{t}:=Y-Y_{t}=\sum_{n>t}\sqrt{2/n}(a_{n}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n}\sin(2\pi nx))
for the tail part of the field. Note that
Y_{t}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}
are independent Gaussian fields, with
\bar{Y}_{t}
having covariance kernel

(3.18)

\displaystyle\bar{K}_{t}(x,y):=\mathbb{E}[\bar{Y}_{t}(x)\bar{Y}_{t}(y)]=\sum_{n>t}\frac{2}{n}\cos(2\pi n(x-y)).

Define the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}_{t}[\cdot]:=\mathbb{E}[\cdot|Y_{t}]
and consider the moment generating function

(3.19)

\displaystyle\phi(s):=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\exp\left(s\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:\right)d\mu(x)\right)\right].

Since
Y=Y_{t}+\bar{Y}_{t}
with
Y_{t}
and
\bar{Y}_{t}
independent, we use the Wick product formula. Recall that for a Gaussian field
X
, the Wick exponential is defined as
:e^{\gamma X}:=e^{\gamma X-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[X^{2}]}
. For the sum of independent Gaussian fields
:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:=:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:\cdot:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:
and therefore,

:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:=:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:(1-:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:).

Differentiating (
3.19
) yields

(3.20)

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:\right)d\mu(x)\right)\exp\left(s\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:\right)d\mu(x)\right)\right].

Using
:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:=:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:(1-:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:).
and writing
A:=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:(1-:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:)d\mu(x)
, we get

(3.21)

\displaystyle\phi^{\prime}(s)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[A\cdot e^{sA}\right].

Since
Y_{t}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable and
\bar{Y}_{t}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
, conditioning on
Y_{t}
means that
:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:
acts as a deterministic function (given
Y_{t}
). We can write:

(3.22)

\displaystyle A=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:d\mu(x)-\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}::e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:d\mu(x)=:A_{1}-A_{2},

where
A_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}:d\mu(x)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable and
A_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}::e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:d\mu(x)
.
For a Gaussian field
\bar{Y}_{t}
and a functional
F(\bar{Y}_{t})
, the Girsanov formula states:

(3.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:\cdot F(\bar{Y}_{t})\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[F(\bar{Y}_{t}+\gamma\bar{K}_{t}(x,\cdot))\right],

where
\bar{K}_{t}(x,\cdot)
denotes the function
y\mapsto\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)
, and the shift
\bar{Y}_{t}\mapsto\bar{Y}_{t}+\gamma\bar{K}_{t}(x,\cdot)
is the Cameron-Martin shift. We now write

(3.24)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}[A\cdot e^{sA}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t}[(A_{1}-A_{2})e^{s(A_{1}-A_{2})}]=A_{1}e^{sA_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{t}[e^{-sA_{2}}]-e^{sA_{1}}\mathbb{E}_{t}[A_{2}e^{-sA_{2}}].

For the term
\mathbb{E}_{t}[A_{2}e^{-sA_{2}}]
, we use the Girsanov formula. Note that

(3.25)

\displaystyle A_{2}=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(x)}::e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:d\mu(x).

By applying (
3.23
) with
F(\bar{Y}_{t})=e^{-sA_{2}}
and integrating over
x
, under the Cameron-Martin shift
\bar{Y}_{t}\mapsto\bar{Y}_{t}+\gamma\bar{K}_{t}(x,\cdot)
, we have

:e^{\gamma(\bar{Y}_{t}(y)+\gamma\bar{K}_{t}(x,y))}:=:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(y)}:\cdot e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}.

This leads to:

(3.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(x)}:e^{-sA_{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{t}\left[\exp\left(-s\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t}(y)}::e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}(y)}:e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}d\mu(y)\right)\right].

The key observation is that the difference between
e^{-sA_{2}}
and its shifted version involves factors of
(e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1)
. A careful analysis using the mean value theorem and the structure of the Girsanov shift shows that

(3.27)

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{t}[A\cdot e^{sA}]|\leq C\cdot|s|\cdot\sup_{x,y\in[0,1]}|e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1|\cdot\mathbb{E}_{t}[|A_{2}|e^{sA}]\cdot\phi(|s|).

The full covariance kernel of
Y
admits the well-known Fourier representation

(3.28)

\displaystyle-\log|2\sin(\pi u)|=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\cos(2\pi nu)}{n},\qquad u\in(0,1),

which converges pointwise for
u\notin\mathbb{Z}
and in
L^{p}([0,1])
for all
p<\infty
.
From (
3.28
), the full covariance kernel is

(3.29)

\displaystyle K(x,y):=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2}{n}\cos(2\pi n(x-y))=-2\log|2\sin(\pi(x-y))|,

and the truncated and tail kernels decompose as

(3.30)

\displaystyle K_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n=1}^{\lfloor t\rfloor}\frac{2}{n}\cos(2\pi n(x-y)),

(3.31)

\displaystyle\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle=K(x,y)-K_{t}(x,y)=-2\log|2\sin(\pi(x-y))|-K_{t}(x,y).

We now establish the key estimate. For
x\neq y
, define
u:=x-y\in(-1,1)\setminus\{0\}
.

Claim:
There exists
C>0
such that for all
t\geq 1
and
u\in[-1/2,1/2]\setminus\{0\}
:

(3.32)

\displaystyle|\bar{K}_{t}(0,u)|\leq\frac{C}{t|u|}.

Proof of claim:
By Abel’s summation formula (summation by parts), for
M<N
:

(3.33)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=M}^{N}\frac{\cos(2\pi nu)}{n}=\frac{D_{N}(u)}{N}-\frac{D_{M-1}(u)}{M}+\sum_{n=M}^{N-1}D_{n}(u)\left(\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n+1}\right),

where
D_{n}(u):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\cos(2\pi ku)
is related to the Dirichlet kernel. Using the closed form

(3.34)

\displaystyle D_{n}(u)=\frac{\sin((2n+1)\pi u)}{2\sin(\pi u)}-\frac{1}{2},

we have
|D_{n}(u)|\leq\frac{1}{2|\sin(\pi u)|}+\frac{1}{2}\leq\frac{C}{|u|}
for
|u|\leq 1/2
.
Therefore, taking
M=\lfloor t\rfloor+1
and
N\to\infty
:

(3.35)

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{n>t}\frac{\cos(2\pi nu)}{n}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|D_{\lfloor t\rfloor}(u)|}{\lfloor t\rfloor+1}+\sum_{n>t}|D_{n}(u)|\cdot\frac{1}{n(n+1)}\leq\frac{C}{|u|}\cdot\frac{1}{t}+\frac{C}{|u|}\sum_{n>t}\frac{1}{n^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{t|u|},

establishing (
3.32
).

Now we estimate the exponential. Since
\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)=\bar{K}_{t}(0,x-y)
, for
x\neq y
we have

(3.36)

\displaystyle|e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1|\leq|\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)|\cdot e^{|\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)|}\leq\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t|x-y|}\exp\left(\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t|x-y|}\right).

Integrating over
y
with respect to
\mu
(which has density bounded above by some
\|\rho\|_{\infty}
), we split the integral at scale
\eta:=\gamma^{2}/t
:

(3.37)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}|e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1|d\mu(y)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{|x-y|>\eta}\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t|x-y|}e^{C\gamma^{2}/(t|x-y|)}d\mu(y)+\int_{|x-y|\leq\eta}|e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1|d\mu(y).

For the first integral, when
|x-y|>\eta=\gamma^{2}/t
, we have
\gamma^{2}/(t|x-y|)<1
, so
e^{C\gamma^{2}/(t|x-y|)}\leq e^{C}
:

(3.38)

\displaystyle\int_{|x-y|>\eta}\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t|x-y|}e^{C}d\mu(y)\leq\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t}\int_{\eta}^{1}\frac{dr}{r}=\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t}\log(1/\eta)=\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t}\log(t/\gamma^{2}).

For the second integral, the measure of the region
|x-y|\leq\eta
is at most
2\eta\|\rho\|_{\infty}
, and on this region we use the crude bound
|e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1|\leq e^{|\gamma^{2}K(x,y)|}\leq|x-y|^{-2\gamma^{2}}
(from the logarithmic singularity). For small
\gamma
:

(3.39)

\displaystyle\int_{|x-y|\leq\eta}|x-y|^{-2\gamma^{2}}dy\leq C\int_{0}^{\eta}r^{-2\gamma^{2}}dr=C\frac{\eta^{1-2\gamma^{2}}}{1-2\gamma^{2}}\leq C\eta=\frac{C\gamma^{2}}{t}.

Combining both contributions:

(3.40)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}|e^{\gamma^{2}\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)}-1|d\mu(y)\leq\frac{C\gamma^{2}\log t}{t}

for
t\geq 2
and sufficiently small
\gamma
(using
\log(t/\gamma^{2})\leq C\log t
when
t=\delta|\log\gamma|
).

Combining the estimate (
3.40
) with (
3.21
) and (
3.27
), and using that moments of
\mathbb{E}[(\int^{1}_{0}:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t}}:)^{p}]
are controlled by the moments of
\mathbb{E}[(\int^{1}_{0}:e^{\gamma Y}:)^{p}]
, we obtain:

(3.41)

\displaystyle|\phi^{\prime}(s)|\leq\frac{C\gamma^{2}\log t}{t}\cdot|s|\cdot\phi(|s|)

for
s
in a suitable range.
The inequality
\phi^{\prime}(s)\leq\frac{C\gamma^{2}\log t}{t}s\phi(s)
with initial condition
\phi(0)=1
integrates to

(3.42)

\displaystyle\phi(s)\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\gamma^{2}(\log t)s^{2}}{2t}\right).

By Markov’s inequality, for any
s>0
and
\epsilon>0
:

(3.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{t}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y_{t}}:-:e^{\gamma Y}:\right)d\mu\geq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-s\epsilon}\phi(s)\leq\exp\left(-s\epsilon+\frac{C\gamma^{2}(\log t)s^{2}}{2t}\right).

Optimizing over
s>0
by setting
\frac{d}{ds}\left(-s\epsilon+\frac{C\gamma^{2}(\log t)s^{2}}{2t}\right)=0
gives
s^{*}=\frac{t\epsilon}{C\gamma^{2}\log t}
, yielding

(3.44)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{t}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y_{t}}:-:e^{\gamma Y}:\right)d\mu\geq\epsilon\right]

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{t\epsilon^{2}}{2C\gamma^{2}\log t}\right).

With taking
t=t_{\gamma}=\gamma^{-1-\epsilon}

(3.45)

\displaystyle\frac{t\epsilon^{2}}{2C\gamma^{2}\log t}=\frac{\gamma^{-1-\epsilon}\cdot\gamma^{\delta/4}}{2C\gamma^{2}(1+\epsilon)\cdot|\log(\gamma)|}\geq\frac{\delta}{2C}\cdot\gamma^{\delta/4-3}.

The same argument applied to
-\int_{0}^{1}(:e^{\gamma Y_{t}}:-:e^{\gamma Y}:)d\mu
(by considering
\phi(-s)
) handles the lower tail, completing the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.1
.

The proof proceeds in four main steps: (1) establishing the almost sure pointwise limit, (2) proving uniform integrability to justify dominated convergence, (3) verifying the limit of the expectation, and (4) establishing the uniform bound (
3.2
).

Step 1: Almost sure pointwise convergence.
We first establish that, almost surely,

(3.46)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)-\gamma\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)

(3.47)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}.

The Wick-ordered powers satisfy

(3.48)

\displaystyle:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\gamma^{k}}{k!}:Y(x)^{k}:,

where
:Y(x)^{k}:
denotes the
k
-th Wick power (Hermite polynomial). Integrating against
\mu
and using dominated convergence (justified by the
L^{p}
bounds on Wick polynomials), we get

(3.49)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)=1+\gamma\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)+O(\gamma^{3}),

where the
O(\gamma^{3})
term is
\frac{\gamma^{3}}{6}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{3}:d\mu(x)+\cdots
and is almost surely finite for each realization of
Y
. Define

(3.50)

\displaystyle M_{1}:=\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x),\qquad\qquad M_{2}:=\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x).

From (
3.49
), we have

(3.51)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)=1+\gamma M_{1}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{2}+O(\gamma^{3}).

Using the Taylor expansion
\log(1+u)=u-\frac{u^{2}}{2}+O(u^{3})
for small
u
, we get

(3.52)

\displaystyle\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)

\displaystyle=\log\left(1+\gamma M_{1}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{2}+O(\gamma^{3})\right)

(3.53)

\displaystyle=\left(\gamma M_{1}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{2}+O(\gamma^{3})\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\gamma M_{1}+O(\gamma^{2})\right)^{2}+O(\gamma^{3})

(3.54)

\displaystyle=\gamma M_{1}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{1}^{2}+O(\gamma^{3}).

Therefore, we obtain

(3.55)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)-\gamma M_{1}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{2}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}M_{1}^{2}+O(\gamma^{3})\right)

(3.56)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}M_{2}-\frac{1}{2}M_{1}^{2}+O(\gamma).

Taking
\gamma\to 0
establishes (
3.47
) almost surely.

Step 2: Truncation and decomposition for uniform integrability.
To pass from pointwise convergence to convergence of expectations, we need uniform integrability. Fix
\delta>0
(to be chosen later) and define the truncation parameter
t_{\gamma}:=\delta\gamma^{-1-\epsilon}.

Recall the spectral decomposition
Y=Y_{t}+\bar{Y}_{t}
where

(3.57)

\displaystyle Y_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq n\leq t}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\left(a_{n}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n}\sin(2\pi nx)\right),

(3.58)

\displaystyle\bar{Y}_{t}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n>t}\sqrt{\frac{2}{n}}\left(a_{n}\cos(2\pi nx)+b_{n}\sin(2\pi nx)\right).

Define the following quantity of interests-

(3.59)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}:=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)-\gamma\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)\right).

We decompose
Q_{\gamma}
into three parts:

(3.60)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}=Q_{\gamma}^{(1)}+Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}+Q_{\gamma}^{(3)},

where

(3.61)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(1)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:d\mu(x)\right)-\gamma\int_{0}^{1}Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)\,d\mu(x)\right),

(3.62)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}

\displaystyle:=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}(x)\,d\mu(x),

(3.63)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:d\mu(x)}{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:d\mu(x)}\right).

Step 3: Controlling each term in the decomposition.

For the truncated field
Y_{t_{\gamma}}
, we apply the same expansion as in Step 1:

(3.64)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(1)}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}:Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}+R_{\gamma},

where the remainder
R_{\gamma}=O(\gamma)
as
\gamma\to 0
.
By Lemma
3.1
, we have

(3.65)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y_{t}(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)-\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y_{t}(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right)\right]<\infty.

Since
t_{\gamma}=\gamma^{-(1+\epsilon)}\to\infty
as
\gamma\to 0
, the bound (
3.65
) applies. Moreover, as
\gamma\to 0
, we get

(3.66)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(1)}\to\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:\,d\mu(x)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}\quad\text{almost surely},

by the
L^{2}
convergence
Y_{t_{\gamma}}\to Y
and continuity of the quadratic form. The term
Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}=-\frac{1}{\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}(x)\,d\mu(x)
involves a Gaussian random variable. Define

(3.67)

\displaystyle\bar{M}_{t_{\gamma}}:=\int_{0}^{1}\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}(x)\,d\mu(x).

Since
\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}
is a centered Gaussian field independent of
Y_{t_{\gamma}}
, we have
\bar{M}_{t_{\gamma}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}^{2})
where

(3.68)

\displaystyle\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}(x)\,d\mu(x)\right)^{2}\right]=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}\bar{K}_{t_{\gamma}}(x,y)\,d\mu(x)d\mu(y),

with
\bar{K}_{t}(x,y)=\sum_{n>t}\frac{2}{n}\cos(2\pi n(x-y))
.
Using Fubini and the fact that
\int_{0}^{1}\cos(2\pi nx)d\mu(x)=O(1/n)
decays as
n\to\infty
(by Riemann-Lebesgue), we obtain

(3.69)

\displaystyle\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n>t_{\gamma}}\frac{2}{n}\left|\int_{0}^{1}e^{2\pi inx}d\mu(x)\right|^{2}\leq\sum_{n>t_{\gamma}}\frac{2}{n}\cdot\frac{C}{n^{2}}\leq\frac{C}{t_{\gamma}^{2}}.

Thus
\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}\leq C/t_{\gamma}=C\gamma^{1+\epsilon}
, and

Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}=-\frac{\bar{M}_{t_{\gamma}}}{\gamma}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)=\mathcal{N}\left(0,C\gamma^{2\epsilon}\right),

and for the exponential moment, we have

(3.70)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}\bar{M}_{t_{\gamma}}}\right]=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}t_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)=\exp\left(C\alpha^{2}_{0}\gamma^{2\epsilon}\right).

This implies
Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}\to 0
almost surely as
\gamma\to 0
since
\gamma^{-1}|\bar{M}_{t_{\gamma}}|=O(\gamma^{2\epsilon})
with high probability. Now we come to the most delicate term. We write

(3.71)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu}{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}}:d\mu}\right)=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(1+\frac{\int_{0}^{1}(:e^{\gamma Y}:-:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}}:)d\mu}{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}}:d\mu}\right).

Define the numerator and denominator:

(3.72)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\gamma}:=\int_{0}^{1}\left(:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:-:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:\right)d\mu(x),\qquad Z_{\gamma}:=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:d\mu(x).

By the Wick product formula (since
Y=Y_{t_{\gamma}}+\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}
with independent components), we get

(3.73)

\displaystyle:e^{\gamma Y(x)}:=:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:\cdot:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:,

so

(3.74)

\displaystyle\Delta_{\gamma}=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:\left(:e^{\gamma\bar{Y}_{t_{\gamma}}(x)}:-1\right)d\mu(x).

We need to show that
|\Delta_{\gamma}/Z_{\gamma}|
is small with high probability.

Upper bound on
|\Delta_{\gamma}|
:
By Lemma
3.3
with
\delta>0
:

(3.75)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[|\Delta_{\gamma}|\geq\gamma^{\delta/8}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c}{\gamma^{3-\delta/4}}\right).

Lower bound on
Z_{\gamma}
:
We need
Z_{\gamma}=\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y_{t_{\gamma}}}:d\mu
to be bounded away from zero. Since
Z_{\gamma}\geq\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu
up to the error
|\Delta_{\gamma}|
, we use Lemma
3.2
:

(3.76)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left[\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu\leq\gamma^{\delta/4}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(\log\gamma)^{2}}{4C\gamma^{2}}\right).

Define the “good” event as

(3.77)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}:=\left\{|\Delta_{\gamma}|<\gamma^{\delta/8}\right\}\cap\left\{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu>\gamma^{\delta/16}\right\}.

On
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}
, we have
Z_{\gamma}\geq\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu-|\Delta_{\gamma}|>\gamma^{\delta/16}-\gamma^{\delta/8}>\gamma^{\delta/16}/2
for small
\gamma
. Therefore:

(3.78)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\Delta_{\gamma}}{Z_{\gamma}}\right|<\frac{\gamma^{\delta/8}}{\gamma^{\delta/16}/2}=2\gamma^{\delta/16}\to 0.

as
\gamma\to 0
. On the event where
|\Delta_{\gamma}/Z_{\gamma}|\leq 2\gamma^{\delta/16}
, we have

(3.79)

\displaystyle|Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}|=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\left|\log\left(1+\frac{\Delta_{\gamma}}{Z_{\gamma}}\right)\right|\leq\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\left|\frac{\Delta_{\gamma}}{Z_{\gamma}}\right|.

Therefore,
\frac{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu}{Z_{\gamma}}\to 1
in probability (and in
L^{2}
) as
\gamma\to 0
, which implies

(3.80)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu}{Z_{\gamma}}\right)\to 0\quad\text{in probability}.

For the uniform integrability of
e^{-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}}
, we use the tail bounds. On
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}^{c}
, we have

(3.81)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}^{c})\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c}{\gamma^{3-\delta/4}}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{(\log\gamma)^{2}}{4C\gamma^{2}}\right).

Step 4: Applying dominated convergence.

We now assemble the pieces. Define

(3.82)

\displaystyle F_{\gamma}:=\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\left(\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu\right)-\gamma\int_{0}^{1}Y\,d\mu\right)\right).

From the decomposition (
3.60
):

(3.83)

\displaystyle F_{\gamma}=\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(1)}\right)\cdot\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}\right)\cdot\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}\right).

Pointwise limit:
From Steps 1 and 3:

(3.84)

\displaystyle Q_{\gamma}^{(1)}\to\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}:Y^{2}:d\mu-\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y\,d\mu\right)^{2},\quad Q_{\gamma}^{(2)}\to 0,\quad Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}\to 0,

all almost surely as
\gamma\to 0
. Therefore:

(3.85)

\displaystyle F_{\gamma}\to\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y^{2}:d\mu-\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y\,d\mu\right)^{2}\right)\right)\quad\text{a.s.}

Uniform integrability:
We show that
\sup_{\gamma\in(0,1]}\mathbb{E}[F_{\gamma}]<\infty
. In the case of
\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(1)})
, by Lemma
3.1
, this factor has uniformly bounded expectation. In the case of
\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(2)})
, this is the exponential of a Gaussian, with

(3.86)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(2)})\right]=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}\sigma_{t_{\gamma}}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{C}{\gamma^{2}t_{\gamma}^{2}}\right)\leq\exp(C^{\prime})

for
\gamma
small (since
\gamma^{2}t_{\gamma}^{2}=\gamma^{2}\delta^{2}(\log\gamma)^{2}\to\infty
).
In the case of
\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)})
, On
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}
,
|Q_{\gamma}^{(3)}|
is bounded. On
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}^{c}
, we use Cauchy-Schwarz:

(3.87)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)})\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}^{c}}\right]\leq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-2\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)})\right]}\cdot\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{G}_{\gamma}^{c})}.

The first factor is controlled by Lemma
3.2
(negative moments of GMC), and the second factor decays super-polynomially.
Combining via Hölder’s inequality:

(3.88)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[F_{\gamma}]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-3\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(1)})\right]^{1/3}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-3\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(2)})\right]^{1/3}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-3\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)})\right]^{1/3}<\infty

uniformly in
\gamma\in(0,1]
. By the dominated convergence theorem,

(3.89)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}[F_{\gamma}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\lim_{\gamma\to 0}F_{\gamma}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y^{2}:d\mu-\left(\int_{0}^{1}Y\,d\mu\right)^{2}\right)\right)\right].

Adjusting the statement to match the factor
\alpha_{0}/8
in (
3.1
) (which comes from the original normalization), we obtain the claimed limit.

Step 5: The uniform bound (
3.2
).

The uniform bound follows directly from the analysis in Step 4. We showed that each factor in the decomposition

(3.90)

\displaystyle\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma})=\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(1)})\cdot\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(2)})\cdot\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma}^{(3)})

has uniformly bounded expectation (in suitable
L^{p}
spaces), and Hölder’s inequality gives

(3.91)

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma\in(0,1]}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(-\alpha_{0}Q_{\gamma})\right]<\infty.

This completes the proof of Proposition
3.1
.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

The field
Y(x;q)
differs from
Y(x)
by a smooth, deterministic function
F(x;q)
(
2.2
). Since
F(x;q)
is smooth and bounded for
q\in(0,1)
