, all the estimates in Lemmas 3.1–3.3 carry over with constants that depend continuously on
q
. Note that the logarithmic singularity of the covariance, which drives the GMC behavior, is the same for
Y(x;q)
and
Y(x)
. Thus Proposition 3.1 applies to both cases with uniform bounds for
q
in compact subsets of
(0,1)
.”

4.
Semi-classical limits for deformed conformal blocks

In this section, we carry out the analysis for the semi-classical limit of deformed conformal blocks (
2.4
) in two cases, for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
and
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
. It is notable that the structure of the conformal blocks in each of these cases is different, and in the limit
\gamma\to 0
, the BPZ equation satisfied by the deformed conformal block reduces to the following equations:

•

For
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
, it reduces to the Hamilton-Jacobi equation of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model, (see Theorem
4.1
).

•

For
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
, it reduces to the Lamé equation, (see Theorem
4.2
).

As a result of these two reductions, the
accessory parameter
of the Lamé equation
[
9
]
and the action from the Hamilton-Jacobi equation can be related by using the semi-classical analogue of the connection (see (
2.21
)) between the undeformed conformal block and deformed conformal blocks for different values of
\chi
.

The above results, while interesting on their own, are vital to prove the existence of the semi-classical limit of the undeformed conformal block, which we study in Section
5
. Furthermore, the relation between the semi-classical limits of the deformed and undeformed conformal blocks as revealed in Theorem
4.2
and relation (
4.157
), (
4.158
) enables us to prove the structure of the conformal block (
1.4
) in Theorem
1.1
.

4.1.
Semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal blocks for
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
and the Hamilton-Jacobi equation

In this section, we study the semi-classical limit of the deformed block
\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi}(z,q)
when the deformation parameter
\chi
is equal to
\frac{2}{\gamma}
. We show that in this case, the semi-classical limit is given by the solution of a Hamilton-Jacobi equation with the potential given by the Weierstrass
\wp
-function as described in the physics literature
[
40
,
9
]
. Our proof heavily relies on the BPZ equation satisfied by the
\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi}(z,q)
and the convergence results established in Section
3
. In what follows, we first state the main result (Theorem
4.1
) of this subsection. Before proceeding to the proof, we state and prove two main tools in Proposition
4.1
and Proposition
4.2
. These two propositions claim and prove the boundedness and the tightness of the sequence of the deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha}_{2/\gamma}(z,q)
as
\gamma\to 0
. The proof of Theorem
4.1
will be detailed in Section
4.1.1
.

Theorem 4.1
.

For
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
,
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
q\in(0,1)
, and
z\in\{u\in\mathbb{C}:0\leq\mathrm{Im}(u)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}\cap\{\mathrm{Re}(z)\in[M,1-M]\}
for any
0<M<\frac{1}{2}
, the semi-classical limit of deformed conformal blocks exists and assumes the form
3
3

3

Note that the range of
\alpha_{0}
here is simply the
\gamma\to 0
limit of the range of values of
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
for which the deformed conformal block is defined (see Definition
2.4
).

(4.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=:\widetilde{\phi}(z,q),

where
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)
could be expressed as

(4.2)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\gamma}{2\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right)+(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\log\Xi

(4.3)

\displaystyle+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]+\xi(z,q),

where
\Psi
,
\Xi
and
h_{\Psi}
are defined in (
4.81
) and (
4.82
) respectively. The function
\xi(z,q)
(explicit in
z,q
) is defined as follows:

(4.4)

\displaystyle\xi(z,q)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+1)}{2}-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}\log(2\pi)+2P_{0}z\pi+\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24}\right)\log q

(4.5)

\displaystyle\quad-(2-\alpha_{0})\log(e^{\mathbf{i}\pi z}\eta(q)^{-3}\theta_{1}(z))+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\left(\frac{4\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.6)

\displaystyle\quad-4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}}{n(1-q^{2n})^{2}}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})^{2}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\left(\frac{8q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}\right).

Furthermore,
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)
satisfies the following partial differential equation which arises as the semi-classical limit of the BPZ equation (
2.22
):

(4.7)

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau))^{2}-(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}\wp(z)+2\pi\mathbf{i}\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)=0,

which is the Hamilton-Jacobi equation for the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model (see
Definition
4.1
) with
\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)
describing the action.

Notice that
\theta_{1}(z+x)
and
\theta_{1}(z)
share the same principle branch for
z\in{u:0<\mathrm{Im}(u)<\mathrm{Im}(\tau)}

[
24
, Lemma 3.1]
. Hence
\log\int^{1}_{0}\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}{|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx
is analytic function of
z
as
z
varies in
{u:0<\mathrm{Im}(u)<\mathrm{Im}(\tau)}
. Therefore the expression in the second line of (
4.7
) is defined through principle branch of logarithm.

Furthermore, In equation (
4.7
),
\partial_{z}
denotes the complex derivative with respect to
z
, where
z
varies in the domain
\{u\in\mathbb{C}:0<\mathrm{Im}(u)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}\cap\{\mathrm{Re}(u)\in[M,1-M]\}
for any
0<M<1/2
. The function
\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)
is analytic in
z
in this domain (as established in Proposition 4.2), so the complex derivative is well-defined.

The derivative
\partial_{\tau}
is the complex derivative with respect to
\tau
, evaluated along the imaginary axis
\tau\in\mathbf{i}\mathbb{R}_{>0}
. Since
\widetilde{\phi}
extends analytically to a neighborhood of the imaginary axis in
\mathbb{H}
, this derivative is also well-defined.

Definition 4.1
.

The non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model is defined by the equations of motion
[
41
]
(see also Appendix
B
for a brief description of the model)

(4.8)

\displaystyle v(\tau)=2\pi\mathbf{i}\frac{du(\tau)}{d\tau},

\displaystyle 2\pi\mathbf{i}\frac{dv(\tau)}{d\tau}=m^{2}\wp^{\prime}(2u(\tau)),

of the Hamiltonian

(4.9)

\displaystyle H(\tau)=v(\tau)^{2}-m^{2}\wp(2u(\tau)|\tau).

The solution
u(\tau)
depends on
\tau
,
m
, and the initial conditions or equivalently, on the monodromy data through the Riemann-Hilbert correspondence
[
11
,
17
]
.
The equation (
4.7
) is then the Hamilton-Jacobi equation of the system above with the identification

(4.10)

\displaystyle z=2u(\tau),

\displaystyle m^{2}=\left(2-\alpha_{0}\right)^{2}.

Lemma 4.1
.

Given initial condition at
\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty
, the solution of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation is unique and coincides with the classical action
[
4
]
corresponding to the Hamiltonian above (see
[
9
]
,
[
13
, p. 258]
)

(4.11)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(2u(\tau),\tau)=\int_{\mathbf{i}\infty}^{\tau}\left(v(\tau^{\prime})^{2}+m^{2}\wp(2u(\tau^{\prime})|\tau^{\prime})\right)\frac{d\tau^{\prime}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
,
q\in(0,1)
, and
z\in\mathcal{B}:=\{u\in\mathbb{C}:0\leq\mathrm{Im}(u)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}
. For parameters
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
and
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z;q)
defined in (
2.18
) satisfies the following uniform bounds:

(4.12)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z;q)\right|

\displaystyle<\infty,

(4.13)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z;q)\right|

\displaystyle>-\infty.

Moreover, these bounds are uniform for
z
and
q
varying over compact subsets of
\mathcal{B}
and
(0,1)
respectively.

Proof.

We divide the proof into four main steps. In Step 1, we analyze the structure of the deformed conformal block and identify the leading-order prefactors. In Step 2, we establish the upper bound (
4.12
) using Hölder’s inequality, negative moment bounds, and the exact moment formula. In Step 3, we prove the upper bound for the case
\alpha_{0}<2
using Selberg integral asymptotics. Finally, in Step 4, we establish the lower bound (
4.13
) using Jensen’s inequality.

Step 1: Structure of the Deformed Conformal Block.

Recall from Definition
2.4
that the deformed conformal block is given by

(4.14)

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,\tau)=\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,q)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right],

where the function inside the expectation

(4.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,q):=\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{1}(z+x)^{\frac{\gamma\chi}{2}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{\gamma\chi}{2}}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx,

and the prefactor
\mathcal{W}(q)
is given by (
2.19
). Under the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,
P=P_{0}/\gamma
, and
\chi=2/\gamma
, we compute the parameter
l_{\chi}
:

(4.16)

\displaystyle l_{\chi}=\frac{\chi^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha\chi}{2}=\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}=\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}.

Similarly, the exponent in the expectation becomes

(4.17)

\displaystyle-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}=-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}=\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}.

Step 1a - Analysis of the prefactor
\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}
:
Substituting (
4.16
) into the expression for
\mathcal{W}(q)
, we compute each of the terms. For the power of
q
in (
2.19
):

(4.18)

\displaystyle\frac{P^{2}}{2}+\frac{\gamma l_{\chi}}{12\chi}-\frac{l_{\chi}^{2}}{6\chi^{2}}=\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{24}-\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24\gamma^{2}}.

For the power of
\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)
:

(4.19)

\displaystyle-\frac{2l_{\chi}^{2}}{3\chi^{2}}+\frac{l_{\chi}}{3}+\frac{4l_{\chi}}{3\gamma\chi}=\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})(4+\alpha_{0})}{6\gamma^{2}}.

Combining these calculations, we get

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}=q^{\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}-\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24\gamma^{2}}+\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{24}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})(4+\alpha_{0})}{6\gamma^{2}}}e^{\frac{2P_{0}z\pi}{\gamma^{2}}}.

Taking the logarithm and multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}\right|

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24}\right)\log q+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})(4+\alpha_{0})}{6}\log|\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)|

(4.21)

\displaystyle\quad+2P_{0}\mathrm{Re}(z)\pi+O(\gamma^{2}).

Since
q\in(0,1)
and
z
varies over a compact subset of
\mathcal{B}
, this expression is bounded as
\gamma\to 0
. Therefore, the prefactor contributes a bounded term to
\gamma^{2}\log|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z;q)|
, and it remains to analyze the expectation term.

Step 2: Upper Bound via Hölder’s Inequality and Negative Moment Bounds.

We now establish the upper bound (
4.12
) for all
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
. The approach uses Hölder’s inequality to separate the various factors in the expectation, followed by moment bounds derived from Lemma
3.2
. To begin with, under the scaling
\chi=2/\gamma,\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma,P=P_{0}/\gamma
, the function (
4.15
) becomes

(4.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}dx.

We now analyze the term

(4.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right].

Step 2a: Decomposition of the function
\mathcal{V}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)
.

We write the factor
\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
as a product of a
z
-dependent factor and a GMC-type integral. To this end, we define the
z
-deformed weight function

(4.24)

\displaystyle w_{z}(x):=\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x},

so that

(4.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=\int_{0}^{1}w_{z}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}dx.

One can express the theta function (see (
A.3
)) as
\theta_{1}(w)=\mathfrak{p}(w;\tau)\sin(\pi w)
, where
c_{1}\leq|\mathfrak{p}(w;\tau)|\leq c_{2}
uniformly for
w
in the relevant domain. The
z
-deformed weight function defined in (
4.24
) can then be written as

(4.26)

\displaystyle w_{z}(x)=\mathfrak{r}_{z}(x)\cdot(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x},

where
\mathfrak{r}_{z}(x)
is a bounded function satisfying

(4.27)

\displaystyle 0<c_{3}\leq|\mathfrak{r}_{z}(x)|\leq c_{4}<\infty

uniformly for
z\in\mathcal{B}
(compact) and
x\in[0,1]
. Here,
c_{i}
,
i=1,...,4
are some constants that are uniform for
q\in(0,1)
and
z\in[M,1-M]
, and
\mathfrak{r}_{z}(x)=\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}\mathfrak{p}(x;\tau)^{-\alpha_{0}/2}
.

Step 2b: Application of Hölder’s inequality.

To bound the expectation (
4.23
), we apply Hölder’s inequality with conjugate exponents
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}>1
satisfying
\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{p_{3}}=1
. We will choose these exponents optimally later. First, we separate the deterministic
z
-dependent prefactor from the GMC integral. Using (
4.26
), we can write (
4.25
) as

(4.28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathfrak{r}_{z}(x)\cdot(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}dx,

and the bounds (
4.27
) then imply that

(4.29)

\displaystyle c_{3}\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,P_{0},\alpha_{0}}\leq\left|\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|\leq c_{4}\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,P_{0},\alpha_{0}},

where

(4.30)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,P_{0},\alpha_{0}}:=\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}dx.

Therefore, in order to study the boundedness of the expectation (
4.23
), it suffices to bound the expression

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,P_{0},\alpha_{0}}^{(2-\alpha_{0})}/{\gamma^{2}}].

To do this, we now proceed using the approach from Proposition
3.1
. Define the probability measure

(4.31)

\displaystyle d\mu(x):=\frac{1}{Z}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx,

where
Z=\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx
is the normalization constant. For
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
, the integral converges and
Z\in(0,\infty)
. With this notation,

(4.32)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\gamma,P_{0},\alpha_{0}}=Z\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}:Y(x;q):}d\mu(x).

Step 2c: Upper Bound for
\alpha_{0}<2
via Selberg Integrals.

We provide a proof of the upper bound for
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
using the explicit representation of moments as Selberg-type integrals. For any positive integer
N
, the
N
-th moment of
\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{N}\right]

(4.33)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\theta_{1}(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x_{i})}{\theta_{1}(z)}|\theta_{1}(x_{i})|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}dx_{i}.

Using the factorization
\theta_{1}(w)=\mathfrak{p}(w;\tau)\sin(\pi w)
, the integral (
4.33
) can be bounded above and below by

(4.34)

\displaystyle c^{\pm\gamma^{2}N(N-1)/4}\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\sin(\pi(x_{i}-x_{j}))|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}|\sin(\pi x_{i})|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}dx_{i},

for some constant
c>0
.

The integral (
4.34
) is a classical Selberg integral with explicit evaluation given by (
2.14
). Taking
N=\lfloor(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}\rfloor+1\sim(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}
as
\gamma\to 0
, a careful asymptotic analysis using Stirling’s approximation shows that

(4.35)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:reduced_integral}=O(N^{2}\gamma^{2})=O\left(\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right).

For
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
, we bound the fractional moment by an integer moment using Jensen’s inequality

(4.36)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|\leq\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{N}\right]\right|

for
N\gamma^{2}\geq 2-\alpha_{0}
. Combining these estimates, we get

(4.37)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|\leq C(\alpha_{0},P_{0})

for some constant
C(\alpha_{0},P_{0})>0
independent of
\gamma
. Together with the prefactor bound from Step 1, this establishes

(4.38)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z;q)\right|<\infty

for all
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
.

Step 3: Lower Bound via Jensen’s Inequality.

We now establish the lower bound (
4.13
).

Step 3a: Application of Jensen’s inequality.

For
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
, the exponent
p:=(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}>0
. The function
h(x)=x^{p}
is convex for
x>0
when
p\geq 1
(which holds for small
\gamma
when
\alpha_{0}<2
). By Jensen’s inequality:

(4.39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{p}\right]\geq\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right]\right)^{p}.

Step 3b: Computation of the first moment.

The first moment is:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}\right]dx

(4.40)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[Y(x;q)^{2}]}dx.

For
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
, the integrand is integrable, and

(4.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right]=I_{\det}(z,q)\cdot(1+O(\gamma^{2})),

where
I_{\det}(z,q):=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\theta_{1}(z+x)}{\theta_{1}(z)}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx
.
Since
z
varies over a compact subset of
\mathcal{B}
and the integrand is continuous and strictly positive:

(4.42)

\displaystyle I_{\det}(z,q)\geq c_{0}>0.

Step 3c: Conclusion of the lower bound.

Combining (
4.180
), (
4.41
), and (
4.42
):

(4.43)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left(c_{0}(1+O(\gamma^{2}))\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}=\exp\left(\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\log c_{0}+O(1)\right).

Taking logarithms and multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
:

(4.44)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|\geq(2-\alpha_{0})\log c_{0}+O(\gamma^{2}).

Since
\log c_{0}
is finite, we conclude

(4.45)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z;q)\right|>-\infty.

This completes the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

Remark 4.1
.

The bounds established in Proposition
4.1
are uniform for
(z,q)
in compact subsets of
\mathcal{B}\times(0,1)
. This uniformity is crucial for the subsequent analysis involving limits and derivatives with respect to
z
and
\tau
.

Remark 4.2
.

Although we stated the proposition for
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
, the actual range of validity for fixed
\gamma>0
is
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2})
. The extension to
\alpha_{0}\geq 2
treated via negative moment bounds demonstrates the flexibility of the Fyodorov-Bouchaud approach.

Proposition 4.2
.

As
\gamma\downarrow 0
, the sequence
\{\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\}_{\gamma>0}
is tight in
C^{2}({[M,1-M]}\times[\epsilon,q_{0}])
for any
0<M<\frac{1}{2}
and
0<\epsilon<q_{0}\in(0,1)
. The parameters satisfy
\alpha_{0}\in(-2,2)
and
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We divide the proof into four main steps. In Step 1, we introduce the Girsanov representation of the deformed conformal block. In Step 2, we perform the asymptotic expansion as
\gamma\to 0
using the Cameron-Martin theorem and Gaussian integration techniques. In Step 3, we establish the commutativity of the limits
\gamma\to 0
and
q\to q_{0}
for any fixed
q_{0}\in(0,1)
. Finally, in Step 4, we prove the tightness in
C^{2}
.

Step 1: Girsanov Representation of the Deformed Conformal Block.

Let us start by rewriting the deformed conformal block (
2.18
) as (see also eq. (3.5) in
[
24
]
)

(4.46)

\displaystyle\psi_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)=q^{\left(\frac{P^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{6\chi^{2}}l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)\right)}\widehat{\psi}_{\chi}^{\alpha}(z,q).

To prove this proposition, it is sufficient to show that the limits
\gamma\to 0
and
q\to q_{0}
commute for
\widehat{\psi}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)
.
The key to proving the commutativity is to express the function
\widehat{\psi}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)
such that the integrand inside the expectation is
q
-independent. Such a structure can be obtained via Girsanov’s theorem (see
[
24
, Theorem C.5]
) that lets us re-express the conformal block in terms of the
q
-independent measure
e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}
, resulting in the following expression:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)=C(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}\theta_{1}(z)^{-l_{\chi}}C_{1}(q)\left(-\mathbf{i}e^{-\mathbf{i}\pi z}q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{l_{\chi}}

(4.47)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)e^{\chi\mathcal{Y}(z,q)}e^{\chi\mathcal{X}(z,q)-\frac{\chi^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi\gamma Px}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right],

where the functions are defined as

(4.49)

\displaystyle\begin{split}C(q)&:=q^{\frac{\gamma l_{\chi}}{12\chi}-\frac{1}{6}\frac{l_{\chi}^{2}}{\chi^{2}}-\frac{1}{6\chi^{2}}l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)}\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)^{-\frac{2l_{\chi}^{2}}{3\chi^{2}}+\frac{l_{\chi}}{3}+\frac{4l_{\chi}}{3\gamma\chi}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha\gamma(-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma})},\\
C_{1}(q)&:=\left(q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\alpha(\alpha-\chi)/2}e^{(\frac{\alpha\gamma}{8}-\frac{\gamma\chi}{8}-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8})\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]},\\
\mathcal{Q}(q)&:=\exp\left[\sqrt{2}\sum_{m,n=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}(a_{n,m}a_{n}+b_{n,m}b_{n})-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}a_{m,n}\right)^{2}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}b_{m,n}\right)^{2}\right],\\
F(0;q)&:=2\sum_{n,m\geq 1}\frac{q^{nm}}{\sqrt{n}}a_{n,m},\\
\mathcal{X}(z,q)&:=-\sqrt{2}\sum_{n,m=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{(2m-1)n}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)a_{n}-\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)b_{n}\right),\\
\mathcal{Y}(z,q)&:=2\sum_{m,n,k\geq 1}a_{n,m}\frac{q^{(2k-1+m)n}}{\sqrt{n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)-2\sum_{m,n,k\geq 1}b_{n,m}\frac{q^{(2k-1+m)n}}{\sqrt{n}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n).\end{split}

The representation (
) arises from applying the Cameron-Martin theorem (Girsanov’s theorem) to factor the
q
-dependence from the GMC integral. Specifically: the field
Y(x;q)
decomposes as
Y(x;q)=Y(x)+F(x;q)
, where
Y(x)
is the
q
-independent field and
F(x;q)
encodes the
q
-dependent modes, the factor
e^{\frac{\alpha}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}
arises from evaluating the
q
-dependent part at the insertion point, the term
\mathcal{Q}(q)
is a correction factor from the change of measure, satisfying
\mathcal{Q}(q)=1+O(q)
, the factors
\mathcal{X}(z,q)
and
\mathcal{Y}(z,q)
account for the
z
-dependence introduced by the deformation, and the constants
C(q)
and
C_{1}(q)
collect the deterministic prefactors.
This factorization is the key step that allows us to extend the analysis to complex
q
and to separate the leading-order
O(\gamma^{-2})
terms. We refer to
[
24
, Lemma 3.6]
for the derivation.

For convenience we define quantity

(4.50)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q):=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)+\chi(\mathcal{X}(z,q)+\mathcal{Y}(z,q)).

With these definitions, equation (
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)

\displaystyle=C(q)e^{\chi Pz\pi}\theta_{1}(z)^{-l_{\chi}}C_{1}(q)\left(-\mathbf{i}e^{-\mathbf{i}\pi z}q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\frac{\chi}{2}(\chi-\alpha)}e^{-\frac{\chi^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}

(4.51)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi\gamma Px}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right].

Step 2: Asymptotic Expansion for
\chi=2/\gamma
.
We now carry out the asymptotic analysis for the deformed conformal block as
\gamma\to 0
. Under the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,
P=P_{0}/\gamma
, and
\chi=2/\gamma
, let us recall the expression (
4.16
) and (
4.17
) which state that
l_{\chi}=\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}
, and the exponent in the GMC integral is
\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}
respectively.

Step 2a: Introduction of auxiliary Gaussian variables.

We introduce auxiliary random variables to simplify the analysis. Define

(4.52)

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n}:=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{n,m}q^{nm},\qquad T^{(2)}_{n}:=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}b_{n,m}q^{nm}.

These are independent Gaussian random variables with distributions

(4.53)

\displaystyle T^{(1)}_{n}\sim N\left(0,\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}\right),\qquad T^{(2)}_{n}\sim N\left(0,\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}\right).

Using these definitions, the function
F(0;q)
in (
4.49
) can be expressed as

(4.54)

\displaystyle F(0;q)=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{T^{(1)}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}},

and
\mathcal{Q}(q)
in (
4.49
) takes the form

(4.55)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(q)=\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sqrt{2}a_{n}T^{(1)}_{n}+\sqrt{2}b_{n}T^{(2)}_{n}-(T^{(1)}_{n})^{2}-(T^{(2)}_{n})^{2}\right)\right).

The functions
\mathcal{X}(z,q)
and
\mathcal{Y}(z,q)
in (
4.49
) can similarly be expressed in terms of
T_{n}^{(1)}
and
T_{n}^{(2)}
in (
4.53
) as

(4.56)

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}(z,q)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n}}\left(a_{n}\cos\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)-b_{n}\sin\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)\right),

and

(4.57)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}(z,q)

\displaystyle=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n}}\left(T^{(1)}_{n}\cos\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)-T^{(2)}_{n}\sin\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)\right).

Substituting the expressions (
4.54
), (
4.57
) in (
4.50
) and considering
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
, we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\gamma}\mathcal{X}(z,q)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{n}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}+\frac{4q^{n}}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})}\cos\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)\right)

(4.58)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{n}^{(2)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{4q^{n}}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})}\sin\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)\right).

Note that
\mathcal{Y}(z,q)
and
Q(q)
depend only on the random variables
\{(T^{(1)}_{n},T^{(2)}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
, while
\mathcal{X}(z,q)
is a function of
\{(a_{n},b_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
. Substituting (
4.55
), (
4.58
) into the expectation term in (
4.51
), we have

(4.59)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi\gamma Px}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right]

(4.60)

\displaystyle\quad=\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\Bigg(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}-\left((T^{(1)}_{n})^{2}+(T^{(2)}_{n})^{2})\right)+\frac{T_{n}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}+\frac{4q^{n}}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})}\cos\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)+a_{n}\sqrt{2n}\right)

(4.61)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{n}^{(2)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{4q^{n}}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})}\sin\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)+b_{n}\sqrt{2n}\right)\Bigg)

(4.62)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\mathcal{X}(z,q)}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\Bigg].

For the remainder of Step 2, we simplify the above expression by taking the expectation w.r.t
\{(T^{(1)}_{n},T^{(2)}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\{(a_{n},b_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
respectively, thereby leading to the asymptotic expansion of the deformed conformal block.

Step 2b: Integration over
T^{(1)}_{n}
and
T^{(2)}_{n}
.

Noting that
Y(x)
in the expression (
4.62
) is independent of
\{(T^{(1)}_{n},T^{(2)}_{n})\}_{n\geq 1}
, (see also (
2.1
)), we use the identity
\mathbb{E}[e^{xT-T^{2}}]=e^{\frac{x^{2}\sigma^{2}}{2(1+2\sigma^{2})}}/\sqrt{1+2\sigma^{2}}
for
T\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
to simplify (
4.62
) as

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:2g-exp-step1}=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}\right)^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\sqrt{2}a_{n}+\frac{4q^{n}}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)^{2}\right)

(4.63)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\Big(\sqrt{2}b_{n}-\frac{4q^{n}}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\Big)^{2}\right)

(4.64)

\displaystyle\qquad\times e^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\mathcal{X}(z,q)}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\Bigg].

Step 2c: Integration over
a_{n}
and
b_{n}
.

To analyse the dependence of
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
, we define

(4.65)

\displaystyle\Omega_{n}(q):=\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}},

\displaystyle\aleph_{n}(q):=(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})^{-1/2}.

Substitute (
4.56
) in (
4.64
) and simplifying the
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
dependent terms we obtain

(4.66)

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:after_T_integration}=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\aleph_{n}(q)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(a_{n}^{2}+b_{n}^{2}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b_{n}\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.67)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\Bigg].

Recall that
Y(x)
is dependent on the random variables
a_{n}
,
b_{n}
. In order to compute the expectation w.r.t these variables we perform a change of variables to express the above expression in the form
\mathbb{E}[e^{\Psi}f(Y)]
where
\Psi
and
Y
are Gaussian random variables and use the Cameron-Martin theorem.

Let us begin by defining the variables

(4.68)

\displaystyle a^{(1)}_{n}:=a_{n}\Omega_{n}(q)^{1/2},\qquad a^{(1)}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{n}(q))\qquad;

\displaystyle b^{(1)}_{n}:=b_{n}\Omega_{n}(q)^{1/2},\qquad b^{(1)}_{n}\in\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{n}(q)),

with which the expression (
4.67
) simplifies as

(4.69)

(
4.67
)

\displaystyle=\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}\aleph_{n}(q)

(4.70)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.71)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left((a^{(1)}_{n})^{2}+(b^{(1)}_{n})^{2}\right)\right)\exp\left(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}b^{(1)}_{n}\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{\Omega_{n}(q)n}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.72)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a^{(1)}_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)^{1/2}}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n\Omega_{n}(q)}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.73)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\Bigg].

Using the identity

(4.74)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[f(T)e^{xT+T^{2}}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-2\sigma^{2}}}\mathbb{E}[f(\widetilde{T})e^{x\widetilde{T}}],

for random variables
{T}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2})
,
\widetilde{T}\sim N(0,\sigma^{2}/(1-2\sigma^{2}))
, with using
a^{(1)}_{n},b^{(1)}_{n}
in place of
T
and the variables
\widehat{a}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{n}(q)/(1-2\Omega_{n}(q)))
,
\widehat{b}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{n}(q)/(1-2\Omega_{n}(q)))
in place of
\widetilde{T}
,
we further simplify the above expression as

(4.75)

(
4.73
)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.76)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\left(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{b}_{n}\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{\Omega_{n}(q)n}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.77)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{a}_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)^{1/2}}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n\Omega_{n}(q)}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.78)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\Bigg],

The above expression is now in the form
\mathbb{E}[e^{\Psi}f(Y)]
, for Gaussian random variables
\Psi
and
Y
. To make this explicit, let us define the function

(4.79)

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle:=-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{b}_{n}\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{\Omega_{n}(q)n}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)

(4.80)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{a}_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)^{1/2}}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\frac{2\sqrt{2}q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{\gamma(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{n\Omega_{n}(q)}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.81)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}Y(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx,

where

(4.82)

\displaystyle h_{\Psi}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\Psi\cdot Y(x)],\qquad\qquad\Xi:=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx.

The function
h_{\Psi}(x)
represents the conditional expectation of
\Psi
given the Wick-ordered field at
x
, and
\Xi
is a normalization factor.
With these new definitions, the expression

(4.83)

(
4.78
)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.84)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Big[e^{\Psi}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi\gamma Px}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}e^{\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}Y(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\Big]

Using the Cameron-Martin theorem (Girsanov’s formula), the term
e^{\Psi}
inside the expectation can be simplified as follows. For a Gaussian random variable
\Psi
with variance
\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]
,

(4.86)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[e^{\Psi}f(Y)]=e^{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]}\mathbb{E}[f(Y+\mathbb{E}[\Psi.Y(x)])]=e^{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]}\mathbb{E}[f(Y+h_{\Psi})],

where the shift
h_{\Psi}
is applied to the field
Y
.
With the above expression, and defining the measure
d\mu_{\Psi}(x):=\frac{1}{\Xi}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx
, the equation (
) simplifies as

(4.87)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]=\eqref{eq:int-2g-step4}

(4.88)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.89)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]}{2}}\Xi^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\exp\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right)

(4.90)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}d\mu_{\Psi}\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)d\mu_{\Psi}}\Big].

Let us recall that the expression (
4.51
) for
\chi=2/\gamma
, with the scaling
P=P_{0}/\gamma
,
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
, and the expression
l_{\chi}=(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}
, is given by

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}(z,q)=

\displaystyle C(q)e^{2\pi P_{0}z/\gamma^{2}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}C_{1}(q)\left(-\mathbf{i}e^{-\mathbf{i}\pi z}q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}

(4.91)

\displaystyle\times e^{-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\right],

where the factors

(4.92)

\displaystyle C(q)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{def:ell-eta}}e^{\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi(\alpha_{0}-2)\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma^{2}}}(2\pi)^{-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6\gamma^{2}}}\eta(q)^{-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+4)}{2\gamma^{2}}}q^{-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)^{2}}{12\gamma^{2}}},

(4.93)

\displaystyle C_{1}(q)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{1}{8}\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}+\alpha_{0}-2\right)}\eta(q)^{\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma^{2}}}q^{\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\alpha_{0}}{12\gamma^{2}}}.

Substituting (
4.90
), in (
4.91
) and using Proposition
3.1
we obtain the following leading order behaviour of the deformed conformal block as
\gamma\to 0
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}(z,q)

\displaystyle\sim C(q)e^{\frac{2\pi P_{0}z}{\gamma^{2}}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}C_{1}(q)\left(-\mathbf{i}e^{-\mathbf{i}\pi z}q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}

(4.94)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(\frac{1}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.95)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]}{2}}\Xi^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\exp\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right)

(4.96)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{\Psi}(x)-\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{\Psi}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right)\right].

Finally, let us note that the expectation term in (
4.96
) converges as
\gamma\to 0
by Proposition
3.1
. Specifically, since the measure
\mu_{\Psi}
has density bounded below (for
z\in[M,1-M]
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{\Psi}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{\Psi}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right]

(4.97)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{0}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{0}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right],

where
\mu_{0}
is the limiting measure as
\gamma\to 0
.

Step 3: Commutativity of Limits.

We now extract the
\gamma^{2}\log
of the expansion (
4.96
) and take the limit
q\to 0
.

Step 3a: Leading-order terms.

With the expressions (
4.92
), (
4.93
),
\gamma^{2}\log
of the prefactor in (
4.96
) is:

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\left[C(q)e^{\frac{2P_{0}z\pi}{\gamma^{2}}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}C_{1}(q)\left(-ie^{-i\pi z}q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{2}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}\right]

(4.98)

\displaystyle=2P_{0}z\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\log(e^{\mathbf{i}\pi z}\theta_{1}(z))+3(2-\alpha_{0})\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]

(4.99)

\displaystyle-2\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}\log(2\pi)+\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+1)}{2}+O(\gamma^{2}).

Furthermore, from (
4.54
) and (
4.58
) we compute

(4.100)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]=4\sum_{n,m\geq 1}\frac{q^{2nm}}{n},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}\right]=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}}{n(1-q^{2n})^{2}}.

Therefore, using the above expression and the expansion in (
4.96
), we obtain the following leading order behaviour

(4.101)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\widehat{\psi}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}(z,q)

(4.102)

\displaystyle\sim 2P_{0}z\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\log(e^{\mathbf{i}\pi z}\theta_{1}(z))+3(2-\alpha_{0})\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}\sum_{n,m\geq 1}\frac{q^{2nm}}{n}

(4.103)

\displaystyle\quad-4\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}}{n(1-q^{2n})^{2}}-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}\log(2\pi)+\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+1)}{2}

(4.104)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.105)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]+(2-\alpha_{0})\log\Xi+\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\gamma}{2\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right)

(4.106)

\displaystyle\quad+\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{0}(x)-\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{0}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right)\right].

In writing the above expression, we use the fact that
h_{\Psi}
scales as
1/\gamma
as can be seen from (
4.82
), and
\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]
scales as
1/\gamma^{2}
.

Step 3c: Limit as
q\to 0
.

Let us now study the behaviour as
q\to 0
of each of the individual
q
-dependent terms in (
4.106
).

•

From (
A.3
), (
A.6
), we observe that

\lim_{q\to 0}\theta_{1}(z)\eta(q)^{-3}=2\sin(\pi z)
. Therefore, as
q\to 0
,

(4.107)

\displaystyle-(2-\alpha_{0})\log(e^{\mathbf{i}\pi z}\theta_{1}(z))+3(2-\alpha_{0})\log\eta(q)\sim-(2-\alpha_{0})\log(2e^{\mathbf{i}\pi z}\sin\pi z).

•

From (
4.49
), we observe that as
q\to 0
,
F(0;q)\to 0,
and
\mathcal{X}(z,q)\to 0
, and consequently

(4.108)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]\to 0,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}\right]\to 0.

•

The term
\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}\to 0
for all
n\geq 1
, implying that,
\Omega_{n}\to 0
as
q\to 0
, as can be seen from (
4.65
). Moreover, since the variance
\mathbb{E}[\widehat{a}_{n}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{b}_{n}^{2}]=\Omega_{n}(q)/(1-2\Omega_{n}(q))
, the expressions
\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]
,
h_{\Psi}
are bounded as
q\to 0
.

Using the above expressions and noting that the final term in (
4.106
) is
q
-independent, we obtain:

(4.109)

\displaystyle\lim_{q\to 0}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\widehat{\psi}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=2P_{0}z\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\log|2\sin(\pi z)|+C_{2}(z,\alpha_{0},P_{0}),

where

(4.110)

\displaystyle C_{2}(z,\alpha_{0},P_{0}):=\lim_{q\to 0}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(e^{\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]}\Xi^{-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{\gamma^{2}}}\exp\left(\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx\right)\right)

is a transcendental constant, determined via
\gamma\to 0,q\to 0
limit of
\Xi
and
h_{\Psi}
which are solutions to some fixed point equations.
Each term in this expression is a covariance of Gaussian random variables, which depends analytically on
q
for
|q|<1
. Therefore, the limit as
q\to 0
exists and defines an analytic function of
z
.

Step 3d: Verification of commutativity.

To verify that the limits commute, we compute the limit in the opposite order. Taking
q\to 0
first:

•

The prefactor simplifies:
\theta_{1}(z)\to 2\sin(\pi z)
,
q^{1/6}\eta(q)\to 1
.

•

All the other terms in (
4.106
) are uniformly bounded as
q\to 0
, hence leads to the final expression which corresponds to
q=0
case.

•

Proposition
3.1
imples that

(4.111)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{0}(x)-\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{0}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right)\right]=0.

Then taking
\gamma\to 0
gives the same limiting expression (
4.109
) evaluated at
q=0
. The equality of the two limits follows because the right-hand side of (
4.109
) is analytic in
q
for
|q|<r_{0}
for some
r_{0}>0
and for all
z\in(M,1-M)
. This analyticity, combined with the uniform convergence established via the bounds in Proposition
4.1
, implies that the limits
\gamma\to 0
and
q\to 0
are commutative.

Step 4: Tightness in
C^{2}
.

To establish tightness of the sequence
\{\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\}_{\gamma>0}
in
C^{2}([M,1-M]\times[0,q_{0}])
, we verify the conditions of the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem.

Step 4a: Uniform boundedness.

The uniform boundedness follows from Proposition
4.1
:

(4.113)

\displaystyle\sup_{\gamma\in(0,1]}\sup_{(z,q)\in[M,1-M]\times[0,q_{0}]}\left|\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|<\infty.

Step 4b: Equicontinuity of derivatives.

The derivatives with respect to
z
and
q
can be computed from the expansion (
4.96
). The key observations are:

(1)

The prefactor terms contribute derivatives that are
O(\gamma^{-2})
, but after multiplication by
\gamma^{2}
, these give
O(1)
contributions.

(2)

By the Cameron-Martin representation, the derivatives of
h_{\Psi}(x)
and
\Xi
with respect to
z
and
q
involve covariances of Gaussian random variables, which are uniformly bounded and differentiable w.r.t.
z
and
q
.

(3)

The constant pre-factors in the right hand side of (
4.96
) are analytic in
z
and
q
, with uniformly bounded derivatives after taking logarithm of them and multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
.

(4)

As
\gamma\to 0
, the contribution of the term

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{\Psi}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{0}-2}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{\Psi}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right]

vanishes after taking logarithm and multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
. So it does not contribute to the
z
or
q
derivatives.

For the first
z
-derivative, differentiating under the expectation (justified by dominated convergence using the uniform bounds from Proposition
4.1
).
Similarly, the second derivatives
\partial_{z}^{2}
,
\partial_{q}
,
\partial_{z}\partial_{q}
, and
\partial_{q}^{2}
are uniformly bounded by iterating this argument. By the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, uniform boundedness together with equicontinuity of derivatives up to order 2 implies tightness in
C^{2}([M,1-M]\times[0,q_{0}])
. This completes the proof of Proposition
4.2
.
∎

Remark 4.3
.

The expression (
4.109
) is an analytic function of
q
in some open ball
|q|<r_{0}
for all
z\in(0,1)
. This shows that the convergence established above is uniform on the ball
|q|<r_{0}-\epsilon
for any
\epsilon>0
. The limits
\gamma\to 0
and
q\to 0
are therefore commutative, as claimed.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
be the analytic extension of the deformed conformal block for
z\in\mathbb{C}\cap\{u:0\leq\mathrm{Im}(u)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}\cap\{\mathrm{Re}(z)\in[M,1-M]\}
for any
0<M<\frac{1}{2}
and
|q|<r
for some
0<r\ll 1
. The semi-classical limits of the following
z
and
\tau
derivatives of the conformal block are bounded:

(4.114)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\big|\partial_{z}\log(\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q))\big|

\displaystyle<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\big|\partial_{z}\log(\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q))\big|

\displaystyle>-\infty

(4.115)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\big|\partial_{\tau}\log(\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q))\big|

\displaystyle<\infty,

\displaystyle\quad\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\big|\partial_{\tau}\log(\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\chi,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q))\big|

\displaystyle>-\infty

where
\log(\cdot)
is defined with respect to the principal branch.

Proof.

We divide the proof into three steps. In Step 1, we compute the
z
-derivative using the Girsanov representation from Proposition
4.2
. In Step 2, we compute the
\tau
-derivative. In Step 3, we establish the uniform bounds using the asymptotic expansion techniques.

Step 1: Computation of the
z
-derivative.

Using the form of the conformal block (
4.46
) and (
4.51
), we have for
\chi=2/\gamma
:

(4.116)

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)

\displaystyle=q^{\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{24}\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}(\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+1)\right)}\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q),

where
\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
is given by (
4.51
). Taking the logarithmic
z
-derivative:

(4.117)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=\partial_{z}\log\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q),

since the
q
-power prefactor in (
4.116
) is independent of
z
.

From the expression (
4.51
), we identify the
z
-dependent terms:

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{e^{\frac{2P_{0}z\pi}{\gamma^{2}}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\left(-ie^{-i\pi z}\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}}_{\text{explicit }z\text{-dependence}}

(4.118)

\displaystyle\quad\times\underbrace{e^{-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}}_{\text{variance term}}\times\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]}_{\text{expectation term}}\times(\text{$z$-independent factors})

where
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi\gamma Px}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx
.

Step 1a: Derivative of the explicit prefactor.

The logarithmic derivative of the explicit
z
-dependent prefactor is:

(4.119)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\left[e^{\frac{2P_{0}z\pi}{\gamma^{2}}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\left(-ie^{-i\pi z}\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{2P_{0}\pi}{\gamma^{2}}-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}-\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})i\pi}{\gamma^{2}}.

Multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
:

(4.120)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\cdot\partial_{z}\log(\text{prefactor})=2P_{0}\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}-(2-\alpha_{0})i\pi.

For
z\in[M,1-M]
with
0<M<1/2
, the function
\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}
is bounded since
\theta_{1}(z)\neq 0
in this region. Therefore, the contribution from (
4.120
) is uniformly bounded.

Step 1b: Derivative of the variance term.

For the variance term
e^{-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}
, we have

(4.121)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\left[e^{-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}\right]=-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}].

From the definition (
4.49
), we know

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]=2\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}}{n(1-q^{2n})^{2}}.
Since the expectation if
z
independent, thus we get

(4.122)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\cdot\partial_{z}\log(\text{variance term})=-4\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]=0.

Step 1c: Derivative of the expectation term.
For the expectation term, we use the representation from Step 2 of Proposition
4.2
. The logarithmic derivative is:

(4.123)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{\gamma}\partial_{z}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)\cdot e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]}.

From the definition (
4.50
):

(4.124)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)=2\partial_{z}(\mathcal{X}(z,q)+\mathcal{Y}(z,q)),

since
F(0;q)
is independent of
z
. The derivatives
\partial_{z}\mathcal{X}(z,q)
and
\partial_{z}\mathcal{Y}(z,q)
are Gaussian random variables with variances:

(4.125)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\partial_{z}\mathcal{X}(z,q))^{2}]

\displaystyle=8\pi^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{nq^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}}<\infty\quad\text{for }|q|<r,

and similarly for
\partial_{z}\mathcal{Y}(z,q)
. Repeating step 1-2 of the proof of Proposition
4.2
upto (
4.90
), we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}

\displaystyle\left[\partial_{z}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)\cdot e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma}\mathfrak{G}(z;q,\alpha_{0})\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{\gamma^{2}(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]}{2}}\Xi^{\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}}\exp\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathcal{Z}(z;q,\alpha_{0})\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}d\mu_{\Psi}\right)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)d\mu_{\Psi}}\Big].

where
\mathfrak{G}(z;q,\alpha_{0})
is a deterministic analytic function of
z
, and
\mathcal{Z}(z;q,\alpha_{0})
is a Gaussian random variable with analytic dependence on
z
, but independent of
\gamma
. Recall the expansion of the denominator of (
4.123
) from (
4.90
) and notice that the most of
\gamma
dependent terms in the expansion of the numerator and the denominator of (
4.123
) cancels out except the leading prefactor of
\frac{1}{\gamma}
and the expectations in the last line of the above display. However that term (similar to the last term in the expansion of (
4.90
)) is uniformly bounded as
\gamma\to 0
by Proposition
3.1
. This shows

(4.126)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\cdot\left|\partial_{z}\log(\text{expectation term})\right|=O(1).

Step 1d: Conclusion for
z
-derivative.

Combining (
4.120
), (
4.122
), and (
4.126
):

(4.127)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\partial_{z}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=2P_{0}\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}-(2-\alpha_{0})i\pi-4\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]+O(1).

This expression is uniformly bounded for
z\in[M,1-M]
and
|q|<r
, establishing (
4.114
).

Step 2: Computation of the
\tau
-derivative.

Since
q=e^{i\pi\tau}
, we have
\partial_{\tau}=i\pi q\partial_{q}
. Taking the logarithmic
\tau
-derivative of (
4.116
):

(4.128)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}\log q^{\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{24}(\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+1)\right)}+\partial_{\tau}\log\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q).

Step 2a: Derivative of the
q
-power prefactor.

(4.129)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\log q^{\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{(1-\alpha_{0})}{24}(\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+1)\right)}

\displaystyle=i\pi\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{24}\left(\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+1\right)\right).

Multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
:

(4.130)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\cdot\partial_{\tau}\log(\text{$q$-prefactor})=i\pi\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24}+O(\gamma^{2})\right)=O(1).

Step 2b: Derivative of
\widehat{\psi}
.

From (
4.51
), the
\tau
-dependent terms in
\widehat{\psi}
include:
(1)
the functions
C(q)
,
C_{1}(q)
,
\eta(q)
, which depend on
q=e^{i\pi\tau}
,
(2)
the functions
\mathcal{X}(z,q)
,
\mathcal{Y}(z,q)
,
F(0;q)
,
\mathcal{Q}(q)
,
(3)
the factors
\theta_{1}(z)
and
\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)
, which depend on
\tau
.
For the explicit prefactor terms:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\log\left[C(q)C_{1}(q)(q^{1/6}\eta(q))^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=3(2-\alpha_{0})\partial_{\tau}\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8}\partial\tau\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]+O(1)

(4.131)

\displaystyle=\frac{3(2-\alpha_{0})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\eta_{1}(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8}\partial\tau\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]+O(1).

where
\eta_{1}(\tau)=-2\pi\mathbf{i}\partial_{\tau}\log\eta(\tau)
.
For the
\theta_{1}(z)
term:

(4.132)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\log\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}=-\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}\frac{\partial_{\tau}\theta_{1}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}.

The heat equation for theta functions gives
\partial_{\tau}\theta_{1}(z)=\frac{1}{4\pi i}\partial_{z}^{2}\theta_{1}(z)
, so:

(4.133)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\cdot\partial_{\tau}\log\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}=-\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{4\pi i}\frac{\partial_{z}^{2}\theta_{1}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}=O(1),

which is bounded for
z\in[M,1-M]
.

Step 2c: Derivative of the variance and expectation terms.

For the variance term:

(4.134)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\log e^{-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}=-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}\partial_{\tau}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}].

Computing
\partial_{\tau}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]=i\pi q\partial_{q}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]
, we get

(4.135)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]=4i\pi\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{2n}(1+q^{2n})}{(1-q^{2n})^{3}}=O(1)\quad\text{for }|q|<r.

For the expectation term, by a similar argument to Step 1c:

(4.136)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]=\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}+\frac{\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}\right)e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{1}{\gamma}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]}.

The
\tau
-derivatives of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)
and
\mathcal{Q}(q)
involve derivatives of the Gaussian coefficients with respect to
q
, which are uniformly bounded in
|q|<r
for any
r\in(0,1)
. Applying Hölder’s inequality to bound the numerator by
\mathbb{E}[\big(\frac{1}{\gamma}\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}+\frac{\partial_{\tau}\mathcal{Q}}{\mathcal{Q}}\big)^{(1-\epsilon)/\epsilon}]

(4.137)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\cdot\left|\partial_{\tau}\log(\text{expectation term})\right|=O(\gamma)=o(1).

Step 2d: Conclusion for
\tau
-derivative.

Combining all contributions:

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)

\displaystyle=i\pi\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{3(2-\alpha_{0})}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\eta_{1}(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8}\partial\tau\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]

(4.138)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{4\pi i}\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z)}{\theta_{1}(z)}-4\partial_{\tau}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]+o(1).

This expression is uniformly bounded for
z\in[M,1-M]
and
|q|<r
, establishing (
4.115
).

Step 3: Uniform Bounds.

The bounds (
4.114
) and (
4.115
) follow from the explicit expressions (
4.127
) and (
4.138
). The key observations are:

(1)

All terms involving
\theta_{1}(z)
,
\theta_{1}^{\prime}(z)
,
\theta_{1}^{\prime\prime}(z)
are bounded for
z\in[M,1-M]
since
\theta_{1}(z)\neq 0
in this region.

(2)

The Eisenstein series
E_{2}(\tau)
is bounded for
q=e^{i\pi\tau}\in(0,r)
with
r<1
.

(3)

The sums
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]
,
\partial_{z}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]
, and
\partial_{\tau}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]
are convergent and uniformly bounded for
|q|<r
.

(4)

The expectation terms contribute
o(1)
as
\gamma\to 0
.

For the lower bounds (the
\liminf
statements), we note that the expressions (
4.127
) and (
4.138
) converge to finite limits as
\gamma\to 0
. Since all terms are bounded and the limits exist, the
\liminf
must be finite. This completes the proof of Lemma
4.2
.
∎

4.1.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.1

This proof will be carried out in 4 steps. We begin by writing down the explicit form of the semi-classical conformal block which can be obtained using the asymptotic expansion derived in Proposition
4.2
in Step 1. In Step 2 we show the convergence of the semi-classical conformal block by using Proposition
4.2
. In Step 3 we show that the BPZ equation becomes the Hamilton-Jacobi equation of the NAECM model in the
\gamma\to 0
limit. Using the properties of the solution of Hamilton-Jacobi equation, we prove the uniqueness of the semi-classical conformal block in Step 4.

Step 1: Explicit representation of the semi-classical conformal block.

We begin with the representation (
4.46
) for
\chi=2/\gamma
:

(4.139)

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=q^{\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{24}(1+(2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2})\right)}\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q).

With the expression for
\gamma^{2}\log\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
in (
4.106
) and the above equation we have

(4.140)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)

(4.141)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24}\right)\log q+2P_{0}z\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\log(e^{\mathbf{i}\pi z}\theta_{1}(z))+3(2-\alpha_{0})\log\eta(q)

(4.142)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}\left[F(0;q)^{2}\right]-2\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}\log(2\pi)+\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+1)}{2}

(4.143)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{{n}}+\frac{16q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{8\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)+(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\log\Xi

(4.144)

\displaystyle\quad+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\gamma}{2\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right).

In the above equation we use the expression (
).

Step 2: Tightness and subsequential limits.

Proposition
4.2
implies that
\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
is a tight sequence of functions in
C^{2}([M,1-M]\times[0,q_{0}])
for any
0<M<\frac{1}{2}
and
q_{0}\in(0,1)
. By the Arzelà-Ascoli theorem, any subsequence of
\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
has a further converging subsequence. We now show that all subsequential limits are identical. Let
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)
denote a subsequential limit:

(4.145)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=\widetilde{\phi}(z,q).

Furthermore, due to Lemma
4.2
, the sequences
\gamma^{2}\partial_{z}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
and
\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
are also tight in
C^{2}([M,1-M]\times[0,q_{0}])
.

Step 3: Derivation of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation.

With the subsequential limit (
4.145
), we can pass the BPZ equation (
2.22
) to the limit
\gamma\to 0
. Recall that the BPZ equation for the deformed conformal block reads:

(4.146)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)\wp(z)+2\pi i\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\partial_{\tau}\right)\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=0.

Dividing by
\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
and using the identity

\frac{\partial_{z}^{2}\psi}{\psi}=\partial_{z}^{2}\log\psi+(\partial_{z}\log\psi)^{2},

we obtain

(4.147)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}\log\psi+(\partial_{z}\log\psi)^{2}-l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)\wp(z)+2\pi i\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\partial_{\tau}\log\psi=0.

Multiplying by
\gamma^{4}
and taking
\gamma\to 0
:

(4.148)

\displaystyle\gamma^{4}\partial_{z}^{2}\log\psi+(\gamma^{2}\partial_{z}\log\psi)^{2}-\gamma^{4}l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)\wp(z)+2\pi i\gamma^{2}\cdot\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\partial_{\tau}\log\psi=0.

Using
l_{\chi}=\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}
, we have
\gamma^{4}l_{\chi}(l_{\chi}+1)=(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}+O(\gamma^{2})
. In the limit
\gamma\to 0
, the terms
\gamma^{4}\partial_{z}^{2}\log\psi\to 0
and we obtain:

(4.149)

\displaystyle(\partial_{z}\widetilde{\phi}(z,q))^{2}-(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}\wp(z)+2\pi i\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)=0.

This is precisely the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
4.7
) corresponding to the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model (see Definition
4.1
).

Step 4: Uniqueness of the semi-classical limit.

From the above analysis, any subsequential limit of
\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
solves the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
4.149
). To establish uniqueness, we use two key results:

(1)

Initial condition:
The commutativity of limits
\gamma\to 0
and
q\to 0
established in Proposition
4.2
uniquely determines the initial condition of the Hamilton-Jacobi equation through Theorem
6.3
. Specifically, from the expression (
4.144
) and the
q\to 0
analysis in Step 3 of Proposition
4.2
,
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)
has the following behaviour for
q\to 0
:

(4.150)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)\sim\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}}{24}\right)\log q+2P_{0}z\pi-(2-\alpha_{0})\log|2\sin(\pi z)|+C_{2}(\alpha_{0},P_{0}),

where
C_{2}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is an explicit constant.

(2)

Uniqueness of solutions:
By Lemma
4.1
, the Hamilton-Jacobi equation (
4.149
) with the initial condition (
4.150
) has a unique solution in the class of functions that are analytic in
q
for
|q|<r_{0}
and satisfy the appropriate growth conditions.

Since all subsequential limits of
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)
satisfy the same Hamilton-Jacobi equation with the same initial condition, and the solution is unique, we conclude that all subsequential limits are identical.

Step 6: Conclusion.
By the uniqueness established in Step 5, the limit

(4.151)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{2/\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)=:\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)

exists. Moreover, substituting the expressions (
4.65
) and (
4.100
) in the equation (
4.144
), the limit
\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)
can be expressed as:

(4.152)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\left({\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\gamma}{2\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\Psi}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\Psi}(x)}dx}\right)+(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\log\Xi

(4.153)

\displaystyle+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Psi^{2}]+\xi(z,q)

where function
\xi(z,q)
is defined in (
4.6
). This completes the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

4.2.
Semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal blocks for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
and the Lamé equation

As in the previous section, we study the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block for the deformation parameter
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
. We show that the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block solves Lamé equation as noted in
[
54
]
. Furthermore, we derive relation between the accessory parameter of the Lamé equation and the Hamiltonian mentioned in (
4.9
) (see also
[
9
]
). We first state this semi-classical limit in the following result (Theorem
4.2
). We prove this result after stating and proving Proposition
4.3
which shows the boundedness of the sequence of deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2,P}(z,q)
as
\gamma\to 0
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Consider
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
,
q\in(0,1)
, and the constants
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then, the following limit exists

(4.154)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)e^{-\phi(q,\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}=:\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q),

where
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is given by

(4.155)

\displaystyle\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}+\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\right)\log q-2\alpha_{0}\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}\sum_{n,m\geq 1}\frac{q^{2nm}}{n}-\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}

(4.156)

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{0}^{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Phi^{2}]-\alpha_{0}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}

(4.157)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{q}(x)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right),

where
\Phi
is defined in (
4.217
),
\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx
,
h_{q}(x):=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)
, with
\widetilde{\Psi}
,
h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)
are defined in (
4.198
).

Moreover,
\phi(q,\alpha_{0},P_{0})
has the following properties:

(1)

\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
assumes the role of the accessory parameter of the Lamé equation

(4.158)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-1\right)\wp(z)+\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}{\phi}(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})\right)\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)=0,

where the solution
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is given by

(4.159)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)

\displaystyle=e^{P_{0}z\pi/2}\theta_{1}(z)^{\alpha_{0}/4}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}z/4}\exp\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(q)M_{\gamma}(z,q)\right]+\frac{1}{2}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}-\alpha_{0}\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\right)

(4.160)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(\alpha_{0}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\left(\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}\right)\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.161)

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)-\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}h_{q}(x)\right)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right),

where
\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx
,
\Phi
and
M_{\gamma}(z,q)
are defined in (
4.217
) and (
4.220
) respectively, and
\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)
is the solution to the integral equation (
4.224
).

(2)

Furthermore,
\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is related to the Hamiltonian (
4.9
) of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser model as follows. Let
u(\tau)
be the solution of the equations of motion (
4.8
) with
m^{2}=(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}
, and let
\tau_{\star}
be a zero of
u(\tau)
, i.e.,
u(\tau_{\star})=0
. Then

(4.162)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{\star}}=-\frac{H(\tau_{\star})}{4},\qquad q_{\star}=e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau_{\star}},

where
H(\tau_{\star})\equiv H(\tau_{\star},\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is the Hamiltonian (
4.9
) evaluated at
\tau=\tau_{\star}
.

Note that, from the formula for the solution of the Lamé equation in (
4.161
), we can obtain an alternative expression for the accessory parameter. See Remark
4.4
.

Let us now detail the propositions needed to prove the above theorem.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
,
q\in(0,1)
, and
z\in\mathcal{B}:=\{u\in\mathbb{C}:0\leq\mathrm{Im}(u)<\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\}
. For parameters
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
and
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
defined in (
2.18
) satisfies the following uniform bounds:

(4.163)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|

\displaystyle<\infty,

(4.164)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|

\displaystyle>-\infty.

Moreover, these bounds are uniform for
z
and
q
varying over compact subsets of
\mathcal{B}
and
(0,1)
respectively.

Proof.

We organize the proof into four main steps. In Step 1, we analyze the structure of the deformed conformal block with
\chi=\gamma/2
and identify the leading-order prefactors. In Step 2, we establish the upper bound (
4.163
) using Hölder’s inequality and negative moment bounds via the Fyodorov-Bouchaud formula. In Step 3, we provide an alternative upper bound using Selberg integral asymptotics. Finally, in Step 4, we establish the lower bound (
4.164
) using Jensen’s inequality.

Step 1: Structure of the Deformed Conformal Block.

We begin by rewriting the expression for the deformed conformal block (
2.18
) with
\chi=\gamma/2
, and the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,
P=P_{0}/\gamma
:

(4.165)

\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,\tau)=\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi P_{0}z\pi/2}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\right],

where

(4.166)

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q):=\int^{1}_{0}\theta_{1}(z+x)^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}\theta_{1}(x)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx.

Step 1a: Analysis of the prefactor.

The prefactor in (
4.165
), defined by (
2.19
), simplifies as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi\frac{P_{0}}{\gamma}z\pi}

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{\gamma l_{\chi}}{12\chi}-\frac{1}{6}\frac{l_{\chi}^{2}}{\chi^{2}}}\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)^{-\frac{2l_{\chi}^{2}}{3\chi^{2}}+\frac{l_{\chi}}{3}+\frac{4l_{\chi}}{3\gamma\chi}}e^{P_{0}z\pi/2}

(4.167)

\displaystyle=q^{\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24\gamma^{2}}+O(1)}\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}(4+\alpha_{0})}{6\gamma^{2}}+O(1)}e^{\frac{P_{0}z\pi}{2}},

where we used the expression

l_{\chi}=\frac{\chi^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha\chi}{2}=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}
of
l_{\chi}
in (
A.2
) for
\chi=\gamma/2
.

Taking
\gamma^{2}\log
of this prefactor:

(4.168)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathcal{W}(q)e^{\chi\frac{P_{0}}{\gamma}z\pi}\right|

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\right)\log q-\frac{\alpha_{0}(4+\alpha_{0})}{6}\log|\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)|+O(\gamma^{2}).

Since
q\in(0,1)
and
z
varies over a compact subset of
\mathcal{B}
, this expression is bounded as
\gamma\to 0
. Therefore, to prove this proposition it suffices to analyze the expectation term:

(4.169)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{\frac{\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right].

Step 1b: Comparison with the
z
-independent integral.

We observe that the
z
-dependent factor in (
4.166
) satisfies:

(4.170)

\displaystyle\theta_{1}(z+x)^{\gamma^{2}/4}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\gamma^{2}/4}=e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\log(\theta_{1}(z+x)/\theta_{1}(z))}.

For
z
in a compact subset of
\mathcal{B}
and
x\in[0,1]
, the function
\log(\theta_{1}(z+x)/\theta_{1}(z))
is bounded. Therefore, there exist constants
0<c_{1}\leq c_{2}<\infty
(depending on the compact set but independent of
\gamma
) such that:

(4.171)

\displaystyle c_{1}^{\gamma^{2}/4}\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}\leq\left|\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|\leq c_{2}^{\gamma^{2}/4}\mathcal{I}_{\gamma},

where

(4.172)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}:=\int_{0}^{1}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx.

Since
c_{1}^{\gamma^{2}/4}=1+O(\gamma^{2})
and
c_{2}^{\gamma^{2}/4}=1+O(\gamma^{2})
, the comparison (
4.171
) implies that the boundedness of the expectation term follows from the bounds of the moments of
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
.

Step 2: Upper Bound via Lemma
3.2
.

We now establish the upper bound (
4.163
) by analyzing
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}]
.

Case 1:
\alpha_{0}\in[0,2)
.

For this range of
\alpha_{0}
, the exponent
(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}=1/2-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}<0
for small
\gamma
, so we need to bound negative moments of
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
.
Using the factorization
\theta_{1}(x)=\mathfrak{p}(x;\tau)\sin(\pi x)
where
c_{1}\leq|\mathfrak{p}(x;\tau)|\leq c_{2}
uniformly for
x\in[0,1]
, we have:

\widetilde{c}_{1}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}\leq\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}\leq\widetilde{c}_{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma},

where

(4.173)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}:=\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx.

Recall that
Y(x;q)=Y(x)+F(x;q)
where
F(x;q)
is a smooth Gaussian field and furthermore,
\mathbb{E}[Y(x;q)Y(y;q)]\geq\mathbb{E}[Y(x)Y(y)]-2\log(\prod_{k\geq 1}(1+q^{k})^{2})
