. By Slepian’s inequality, we get
\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}]\leq\prod_{k}(1+q^{k})^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[(\int^{1}_{0}:e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}:d\mu(x))^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}]
. Applying Lemma
3.2
for
\beta>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
to this upper bound, we get

(4.174)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{\gamma}^{-\beta/\gamma^{2}}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\beta^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right),

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
\beta
and
\gamma
.
Taking
\beta=\alpha_{0}-\gamma^{2}/2\approx\alpha_{0}
for small
\gamma
:

(4.175)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\right]\leq C\alpha_{0}^{2}+O(\gamma^{2}).

Case 2:
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,0)
.

In this case, the exponent
(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}=1/2+|\alpha_{0}|/\gamma^{2}>0
, so we need to bound positive moments of
\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}
instead. For any positive integer
N
, the
N
-th moment of
\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
is given by the Selberg-type integral:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{N}\right]

(4.176)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\theta_{1}(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\theta_{1}(z+x_{i})^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}|\theta_{1}(x_{i})|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}dx_{i}.

Notice that
\theta_{1}(w)=\mathfrak{p}(w;\tau)\sin(\pi w)
where
c_{1}\leq|\mathfrak{p}(w;\tau)|\leq c_{2}
as
w
varies in the relevant domain. The
z
-dependent factors
\theta_{1}(z+x_{i})^{\gamma^{2}/4}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\gamma^{2}/4}
contribute multiplicative constants of order
1+O(\gamma^{2})
.
Therefore, the integral (
4.176
) can be bounded above and below by constant multiples of:

(4.177)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq N}|\sin(\pi(x_{i}-x_{j}))|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}|\sin(\pi x_{i})|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}dx_{i}.

This integral has an explicit expression as stated in (
2.14
) of Remark
2.2
. As
N
grows, the ratios of Gamma functions in (
2.14
) grow as
\exp(cN^{2}\gamma^{2})
.
When
|\alpha_{0}|<2
, the fractional moment
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{V}^{N}]
with
N=\lfloor|(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}|\rfloor+1
bounds the desired expectation. The large
N
asymptotics show that:

(4.178)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\right]\right|\leq\exp\left(\frac{c(\alpha_{0})}{\gamma^{2}}\right)

for some constant
c(\alpha_{0})>0
.

Combined upper bound.
Combining Cases 1 and 2 with the prefactor analysis from Step 1, we conclude:

(4.179)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|\leq C(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)<\infty

for all
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
, establishing (
4.163
).

Step 3: Lower Bound via Jensen’s Inequality.

We now establish the lower bound (
4.164
). For
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
and small
\gamma>0
, the exponent
p:=(\alpha_{0}-\gamma^{2}/2)/\gamma^{2}
satisfies
|p|\to\infty
as
\gamma\to 0
. The function
h(x)=x^{-p}
is convex for
x>0
when
p>0
(i.e., when
\alpha_{0}>\gamma^{2}/2
). Applying Jensen’s inequality:

(4.180)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\right]\geq\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right]\right)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}.

The first moment is deterministic:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{1}(z+x)^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}\right]dx

(4.181)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{1}(z+x)^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[Y(x;q)^{2}]}dx.

For
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
, the integrand is integrable on
[0,1]
, and as
\gamma\to 0
:

(4.182)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right]=\int_{0}^{1}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx+O(\gamma^{2})=:Z_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})+O(\gamma^{2}).

Since
z
varies over a compact subset of
\mathcal{B}
and the integrand is continuous and strictly positive for
x\in(0,1)
, we have
Z_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})>0
. Therefore, from (
4.180
):

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\right]\right|

\displaystyle\geq\left(Z_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})+O(\gamma^{2})\right)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}

(4.183)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\log Z_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})+O(1)\right).

Taking
\gamma^{2}\log
:

(4.184)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\right]\right|\geq\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}-\alpha_{0}\right)\log Z_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})+O(\gamma^{2}).

Since
\log Z_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is finite, we conclude:

(4.185)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)^{(\gamma^{2}/2-\alpha_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\right]\right|>-\infty.

Combining (
4.185
) with the prefactor analysis from Step 1, we obtain:

(4.186)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)\right|>-\infty,

establishing (
4.164
). This completes the proof of Proposition
4.3
.
∎

4.2.1.
Proof of Theorem
4.2

We first prove part (1) of Theorem
4.2
. We organize the proof into four main steps. In Step 1, we carry out asymptotic exapnsion of undeformed and deformed conformal block (for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
) following similar steps as in Proposition
4.2
. In Step 2, we perform a refined expansion to identify the correction terms that determine
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
. In Step 3, we derive the Lamé equation from the BPZ equation. In Step 4, we establish the uniqueness of the semi-classical limit.

Step 1: Asymptotic Expansion of the Deformed Conformal Block.

We now carry out an analogous analysis to the proof of Theorem
4.1
for the deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,q)
defined in (
2.18
) for
\chi=\gamma/2
. Recall from (
4.46
) that using Girsanov theorem we can rewrite (
2.18
), for
\chi=\gamma/2
,
l_{\gamma/2}=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}
, and the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,
P=P_{0}/\gamma
as

(4.187)

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha_{0}}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}}(z,q)=q^{\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{2}{3\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\right)\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}+1\right)\right)}\widehat{\psi}^{\alpha}_{\chi,P}(z,q),

where, from (
4.51
),

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}(z,q)=C(q)e^{P_{0}z\pi/2}\theta_{1}(z)^{-\left(\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\right)}C_{1}(q)\left(-\mathbf{i}e^{-\mathbf{i}\pi z}q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{(\gamma^{2}/8-\alpha_{0}/4)}e^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{X}(z,q)^{2}]}

(4.188)

\displaystyle\times\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\right]

where the functions
C(q)
,
C_{1}(q)
,
\mathcal{X}(z,q)
are defined in (
4.49
), and we use the expression (
4.55
) for
\mathcal{Q}(q)
.
Analogous expression to (
4.58
) for
\chi=\gamma/2
is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathcal{X}(z,q)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{n}^{(1)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}+\frac{\gamma q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n})}\cos\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)\right)

(4.189)

\displaystyle\quad-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{T_{n}^{(2)}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\gamma q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n})}\sin\left(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n\right)\right),

where the random variables
T_{n}^{(1)}
and
T_{n}^{(2)}
are defined in (
4.52
).

Carrying out computations analogous to Step 2 in the proof of Theorem
4.1
,
i.e
integrating over the variables
T_{n}^{(1)}
and
T_{n}^{(2)}
and making a series of transformations
(a_{n},b_{n})\to(\widehat{a}_{n},\widehat{b}_{n})
, we obtain the expression

(4.190)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}(z,q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{2\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.191)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\left(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{b}_{n}\frac{\gamma q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{2n\Omega(q)}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.192)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{a}_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\gamma q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{2n\Omega(q)}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.193)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\Bigg],

where
\Omega_{n}(q)
is defined in (
4.65
), the variables
\widehat{a}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{n}(q)/(1-2\Omega_{n}(q)))
, and
\widehat{b}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Omega_{n}(q)/(1-2\Omega_{n}(q)))
. Recall that
a_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
b_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. Moreover, the variables
(a_{n},\widehat{a}_{n})
,
(b_{n},\widehat{b}_{n})
are perfectly correlated and therefore

(4.194)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[a_{n}\widehat{a}_{n}]=\left(\frac{\Omega_{n}(q)}{(1-2\Omega_{n}(q))}\right)^{1/2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[b_{n}\widehat{b}_{n}]=\left(\frac{\Omega_{n}(q)}{(1-2\Omega_{n}(q))}\right)^{1/2}.

Similar to Step 2c in the proof of Theorem
4.1
, we now define the tilted Gaussian variable

(4.195)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}\equiv\widetilde{\Psi}(z,q)

\displaystyle:=\left(-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{b}_{n}\frac{\gamma q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{2n\Omega(q)}}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.196)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{a}_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)}{\gamma\sqrt{n}}+\frac{\gamma q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{2n\Omega(q)}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.197)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widetilde{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}Y(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx,

where

(4.198)

\displaystyle h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}\cdot Y(x)],

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}:=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx.

Applying Cameron-Martin theorem (
4.86
), the following expression simplifies

(4.199)

(
4.193
)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{2\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.200)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}^{2}]}{2}}(\widetilde{\Xi})^{\frac{-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left({\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widetilde{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx}\right)

(4.201)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widetilde{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}}\Big],

where
d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x):={\widetilde{\Xi}}^{-1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx
. Note that in the expressions above,
\widetilde{\Psi}
has
z
-independent terms scale as
1/\gamma
, and
z
-dependent terms depend linearly on
\gamma
. Consequently,

(4.202)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}^{2}]=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}^{2}]\underset{\gamma\to 0}{\sim}\mathcal{O}(1),

and

(4.203)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\left(\gamma\,h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\partial_{z}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}\underset{\gamma\to 0}{\sim}\mathcal{O}(\gamma).

Let us now analyse the asymptotic behaviour of the conformal block. For reasons that will be made clear soon, we retain the
q
-dependent terms of the order
\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{-2})
, and the
z
-dependent terms of the order
\mathcal{O}(1)
.

For
\chi=\gamma/2
,
l_{\gamma/2}=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}
, and the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,

, we obtain the following leading order expressions

(4.204)

\displaystyle C(q)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{def:ell-eta}}q^{-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)\alpha_{0}}{12\gamma^{2}}}(2\pi)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6\gamma^{2}}}\eta(q)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{2\gamma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}^{2}/\gamma^{2}},

(4.205)

\displaystyle C_{1}(q)

\displaystyle=\left(q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]}.

Substituting (
4.201
), (
4.204
), (
4.205
) in (
4.187
), and using Proposition
3.1
, we find the following behaviour near
\gamma\to 0
:

(4.206)

\displaystyle\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2,P}(z,q)

\displaystyle\sim(2\pi)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6\gamma^{2}}}q^{\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24\gamma^{2}}\right)}\eta(q)^{-\frac{2\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]}e^{-\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}}e^{P_{0}z\pi/2}\theta_{1}(z)^{\alpha_{0}/4}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}z/4}

(4.207)

\displaystyle\quad\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{n}(q)\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}{n}}+\frac{\gamma^{2}q^{2n}}{(1-q^{2n})^{2}{n}}+\frac{2\alpha_{0}q^{n}}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)\right)

(4.208)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}^{2}]}{2}}(\widetilde{\Xi})^{\frac{-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\exp\left({\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widetilde{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)\right)\right.

(4.209)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\left.\times\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}:Y(x):d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right].

In the last line of the above display we use Proposition
3.1
which states that the measure
\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}
has density bounded below (for
z\in[M,1-M]
), we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right]

(4.210)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x)^{2}:d\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(x)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y(x):d\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(x)\right)^{2}\right)\right],

where
\widetilde{\mu}_{0}(x)
is the limiting measure of
d\mu_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)
as
\gamma\to 0
.
Furthemore, with the conditions (
4.202
), (
4.203
), we observe that

(4.211)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2,P}(z,q)=0

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\log\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2,P}(z,q)\underset{\gamma\to 0}{\sim}\mathcal{O}(1).

Step 2: Refined Expansion and Identification of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
.

We now aim to obtain the explicit form of the leading and sub-leading terms in the expression (
4.209
). Let us begin with the following asymptotic expansion near
\gamma\to 0
:

(4.212)

\displaystyle h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)=\frac{h_{q}(x)}{\gamma}+\gamma\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2}).

Consequently the measure

(4.213)

\displaystyle e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx=e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3}),

and the function
\widetilde{\Xi}
defined in (
4.198
) behaves as

(4.214)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Xi}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx+\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})

(4.215)

\displaystyle=:\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}+\gamma^{2}\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3}).

Note that
\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}
is
z
-independent while
\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}
is
z
-dependent.
Substituting (
4.213
) and (
4.215
) in (
4.197
), we rewrite
\widetilde{\Psi}
as

(4.216)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Psi}=\frac{1}{\gamma}\Phi(q)+\gamma\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(z,q)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2}),

where

(4.217)

\displaystyle\Phi(q)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\widehat{a}_{n}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}\Omega_{n}(q)}{\sqrt{n}}\right)-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}Y(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx

(4.218)

\displaystyle M_{\gamma}(z,q)

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}(2\Omega_{n}(q)-1)}{(1-q^{2n})\sqrt{2n\Omega(q)}}\left(-\widehat{b}_{n}\sin(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)+\widehat{a}_{n}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)

(4.219)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)Y(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx

(4.220)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}^{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}Y(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx\right)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx\right).

The expressions with the definition
h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}\cdot Y(x)]
implies

(4.221)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)=\mathbb{E}\left[M_{\gamma}(z,q)Y(x)\right].

Substituting (
4.220
) in the above expression results in the following functional equation

(4.222)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)

(4.223)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}^{(0)}(x)-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}y}(2\sin(\pi y))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}K(x,y)\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(y)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(y)}dy

(4.224)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}^{2}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}y}(2\sin(\pi y))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}K(x,y)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(y)}dy\right)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{1}{2}h_{q}(x)}dx\right),

where

(4.225)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}^{(0)}(x)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}\left(1-2\Omega_{n}(q)\right)^{1/2}}{n(1-q^{2n})}\cos\left(2\pi(z+x-\tau/2)\right),

and the kernel
K(x,y):=\mathbb{E}[Y(x)Y(y)]
. The expression (
4.225
) is computed by using the equations (
4.194
).

Similarly, with (
4.216
), the expectation term in (
4.209
) can be written as

(4.226)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\Psi}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(q)^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(q)M_{\gamma}(z,q)\right]+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{2}),

where the term
\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(q)^{2}\right]
is
z
-independent. The term
(\widetilde{\Xi})^{\frac{-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}
behaves as

(4.227)

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\Xi})^{\frac{-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\left({\frac{-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\right)\log(\widetilde{\Xi})\right)

(4.228)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{asymp:Xiexp-g2}}\exp\left(\left({\frac{-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}+\gamma^{2}\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}/\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})\right)\right)

(4.229)

\displaystyle=\exp\left({-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}-\alpha_{0}\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}+\mathcal{O}({\gamma^{2}})\right)

(4.230)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}^{1/2}\exp\left(-\alpha_{0}\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}+\mathcal{O}({\gamma^{2}})\right),

and the exponential term

(4.231)

\displaystyle\exp\left({\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widetilde{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widetilde{\Psi}}(x)}dx}\right)

(4.232)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{eq:asymp-htilPsi-g2}\eqref{asymp:Xiexp-g2}}\exp\Bigg(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\left(1-\gamma^{2}\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{4})\right)

(4.233)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\left(\frac{h_{q}(x)}{\gamma}e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}+\frac{3\gamma}{2}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})\right)dx\right)\Bigg)

(4.234)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma^{2}\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{q}(x)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right)\right)

(4.235)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)-\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}h_{q}(x)+\mathcal{O}(\gamma^{3})\right)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right).

Substituting (
4.226
), (
4.230
), (
4.235
) in (
4.209
) and recalling the identity (
), we arrive at the expressions

(4.236)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\psi_{\gamma/2,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}

\displaystyle=\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})

(4.237)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}+\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\right)\log q-2\alpha_{0}\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]-\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}

(4.238)

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\Omega_{n}(q)\alpha_{0}^{2}}{n}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}[\Phi^{2}]-\alpha_{0}\log\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}

(4.239)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{q}(x)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right).

Substituting (
4.65
) and (
4.100
) in the above expression, we get (
4.157
).
The subleading term

(4.240)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Gamma}(z,q)

\displaystyle=e^{P_{0}z\pi/2}\theta_{1}(z)^{\alpha_{0}/4}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha_{0}z/4}\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{2\alpha_{0}q^{n}\Omega_{n}(q)}{(1-q^{2n}){n}}\cos(2\pi(z-\frac{\tau}{2})n)\right)e^{\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi(q)M_{\gamma}(z,q)\right]}\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}^{1/2}

(4.241)

\displaystyle\quad\times e^{\left(-\alpha_{0}\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\right)}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}\left(\frac{3}{2}\mathfrak{R}_{z,q}(x)-\frac{\widetilde{\Xi}_{1}}{\widetilde{\Xi}_{0}}h_{q}(x)\right)e^{\frac{h_{q}(x)}{2}}dx\right).

Step 3: Derivation of the Lamé Equation.

Recall from Theorem
2.3
that the deformed conformal block
\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2,P}(z,q)
satisfies the BPZ equation:

(4.243)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-l_{\gamma/2}(l_{\gamma/2}+1)\wp(z)+2i\pi\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\partial_{\tau}\right)\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2,P}(z,q)=0.

Under the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,
P=P_{0}/\gamma
, we have:

(4.244)

\displaystyle l_{\gamma/2}=\frac{\gamma^{2}}{8}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4},\qquad l_{\gamma/2}(l_{\gamma/2}+1)=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-1\right)+O(\gamma^{2}).

Substituting the limit (
4.154
) into the BPZ equation (
4.243
):

(4.245)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-l_{\gamma/2}(l_{\gamma/2}+1)\wp(z)+2i\pi\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\partial_{\tau}\right)\left(e^{\phi(q)/\gamma^{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)\right)=0.

The action of
\partial_{z}^{2}
on
e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}
gives

\partial_{z}^{2}\left(e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}\right)=e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\partial_{z}^{2}\widetilde{\Gamma},

since
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is independent of
z
.
The action of
\partial_{\tau}
gives

(4.246)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\left(e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\widetilde{\Gamma}\right)=e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})\cdot\widetilde{\Gamma}+\partial_{\tau}\widetilde{\Gamma}\right).

Substituting into (
4.245
) and dividing by
e^{\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})/\gamma^{2}}
yields

(4.247)

\displaystyle\partial_{z}^{2}\widetilde{\Gamma}-l_{\gamma/2}(l_{\gamma/2}+1)\wp(z)\widetilde{\Gamma}+\frac{i\pi}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})\widetilde{\Gamma}+O(\gamma^{2})=0.

Taking
\gamma\to 0
and using (
4.244
):

(4.248)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{z}^{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-1\right)\wp(z)+\frac{i\pi}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})\right)\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)=0.

This is precisely the Lamé equation (
4.158
) with accessory parameter
\frac{\pi i}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
.

Step 4: Uniqueness of the Semi-Classical Limit.

By Proposition
4.3
(the boundedness result), we know that
\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
is tight as
\gamma\to 0
. Therefore, any subsequence has a further converging subsequence.
Let
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
be any sub-sequential limit:

(4.249)

\displaystyle\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})=\lim_{\gamma_{n}\to 0}\gamma_{n}^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma_{n}}_{\gamma_{n}/2,P_{0}/\gamma_{n}}(z,q)

for some sequence
\gamma_{n}\to 0
.
From Step 3, any such subsequential limit must satisfy the property that
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
defined by (
4.154
) solves the Lamé equation (
4.248
). Since
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is given explicitly by (
), which is independent of the choice of subsequence, and since the solution of the Lamé equation uniquely determines the accessory parameter (see
[
21
]

[
32
, Chapter 4]
), we conclude that
\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is uniquely determined.

Proposition
4.4
showed that the semi-classical limit undeformed conformal block is same as
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
upto some explicit additive factor. Together with the asymptotic results of Section
6
which shows unique limit of
\lim_{q\to 0}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
, the uniqueness of
\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
implies that all subsequential limits of
\gamma^{2}\log\psi^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma/2,P_{0}/\gamma}(z,q)
are the same. Therefore, the limit (
4.154
) exists and
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is uniquely determined by
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
.

This completes the proof of part (1) of Theorem
4.2
. Now we proceed to part (2) of Theorem
4.2
.

Proof of Part (2)

There are three main steps in this proof: relating the function
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
defined in (
4.249
) with the action of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser (NAECM) model defined in (
4.11
), regularizing the action, calculating the
\tau
-derivative of
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
and showing its relation to the Hamiltonian of the NAECM model.

Step 1: Relation between
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
and the NAECM action.

Let us begin by studying the expression (
4.290
) for
\chi=2/\gamma
. The exponent
\frac{\chi}{2}(\chi-\alpha)=\frac{1}{\gamma}(\frac{2}{\gamma}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma})=\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}
, and so the expression (
4.290
) becomes

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\lim_{z\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(\theta_{1}(z)^{\frac{2-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\psi_{\frac{2}{\gamma},P}^{\alpha}(z,\tau)\right)

(4.250)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma-2/\gamma}(q)+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log Z^{(\alpha_{0}-2)/\gamma}_{\gamma,P}+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{W}(q).

Let us study the LHS and RHS of the above expression separately.

We begin by expanding the terms in the RHS of the expression above.
Firstly, using the expression (
4.289
), the undeformed conformal block with parameter shift
\alpha_{0}\to\alpha_{0}-2
gives:

(4.251)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{(\alpha_{0}-2)/\gamma}(q)

\displaystyle=\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})-\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}\log q-\left(\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)^{2}}{2}-2(\alpha_{0}-2)\right)\log\eta(q)

(4.252)

\displaystyle+\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)(\alpha_{0}+2)}{6}\log(2\pi)-\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0}).

As in the proof of the previous proposition, we ignore the
q
-independent term
\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})
.
Secondly, shifting the value of
\alpha_{0}\to\alpha_{0}-2
in (
4.293
) we get

(4.253)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log Z^{(\alpha_{0}-2)/\gamma}_{\gamma,P}=\frac{(\alpha_{0}-2)^{2}}{24}\log q+(\alpha_{0}-2)^{2}\log\eta(q)+\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0}).

Finally from (
4.20
), we get

(4.254)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{W}^{2/\gamma}(q)=(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24})\log q-{\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}}\log\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)

(4.255)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{def:ell-eta}}(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24})\log q-{\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{2}}\log\eta(q)-{\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}}\log(2\pi)

Summing the expressions (
4.253
), (
4.252
), and (
4.255
) we get

(4.256)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log Z^{(\alpha_{0}-2)/\gamma}_{\gamma,P}+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{(\alpha_{0}-2)/\gamma}(q)+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{W}^{2/\gamma}(q)

(4.257)

\displaystyle=\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})-6(\alpha_{0}-1)\log\eta(q)-\frac{2(\alpha_{0}+1)}{3}\log(2\pi).

The LHS of the expression (
4.250
), with the equation (
4.145
) reads,

(4.258)

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(\theta_{1}(z)^{\frac{\chi}{2}\left(\chi-\alpha\right)}\psi^{\alpha}_{2/\gamma}(z,\tau)\right)\mathop{=}\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)+(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{z\to 0}\log\theta_{1}(z).

The equality of the expressions (
4.257
) and (
4.258
) implies, with some re-arranging of terms,

(4.259)

\displaystyle\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})-\frac{2(\alpha_{0}+1)}{3}\log(2\pi)=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)+(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{z\to 0}\log\theta_{1}(z)+6(\alpha_{0}-1)\log\eta(q).

Let us recall that by the Definition
4.1
,
\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)
is associated to the action of the non-autonomous elliptic Calogero-Moser (NAECM) model with the identification
z=2u(\tau)
and
m^{2}=(2-\alpha_{0})^{2}
. We now focus on the
z\to 0
limit of the expression above. By Lemma
4.1
, the action is

(4.261)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(2u(\tau),\tau)=\int_{\mathbf{i}\infty}^{\tau}\left(v(\tau^{\prime})^{2}+m^{2}\wp(2u(\tau^{\prime})|\tau^{\prime})\right)\frac{d\tau^{\prime}}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}.

We define the integrand above, which is the Lagrangian of the NAECM model, as

(4.262)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\tau):=\frac{1}{2\pi\mathbf{i}}\left(v(\tau)^{2}+m^{2}\wp(2u(\tau)|\tau)\right).

The identification
z=u(\tau)
implies that the limit
z\to 0
is equivalent to the limit
u(\tau)\to 0
, and therefore, the term
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\widetilde{\phi}(z,\tau)+(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{z\to 0}\log\theta_{1}(z)
is equivalent to studying the expression
\widetilde{\phi}(2u(\tau),\tau)+(2-\alpha_{0})\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))
near the zeros of
u(\tau)
, which we denote by
u(\tau_{\star})=0
. Such an analysis was carried out in
[
9
, Section 7]
, which required an additional regularization of the action (
4.261
) as we will show, it is ill defined at
u(\tau)=0
. In what follows, we will repeat the structure of the computations in
[
9
, Section 7]
to highlight that the term
(2-\alpha_{0})\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))
regularizes our action in a direct manner, and resultantly, we can connect the function
\phi(q,\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})
in (
) to the Hamiltonian of the NAECM model.

Step 2: Regularizing the action

To this end, we first recall that the Weierstrass
\wp
-function behaves as
\wp(z)\sim 1/z^{2}+\mathcal{O}(z^{2})
near
z=0
, and as
\wp(z)\sim\frac{\pi^{2}}{\sin^{2}\pi z}-\frac{\pi^{2}}{3}+16\pi^{2}e^{\mathbf{i}\pi\tau}\sin^{2}(\pi z)
near
z\to\mathbf{i}\infty

[
9
, eq (A.14)]
. As we will show below, this behaviour of
\wp(z)
implies that the function
\mathcal{L}(\tau)
is constant near
\mathbf{i}\infty
, and has a pole neat
\tau=\tau_{\star}
. Indeed, recall that the expansion of
u(\tau)
near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
as specified in Proposition
B.1
, in our notation
4
4

4

{\sf a}=P_{0}
parametrizes the A-cycle monodromy.
is

(4.263)

\displaystyle u(\tau)=P_{0}\tau+{\sf b}+\frac{m^{2}}{8\pi\mathbf{i}P_{0}^{2}}e^{4\pi\mathbf{i}(P_{0}\tau+{\sf b})}+\mathcal{O}(qe^{4\pi\mathbf{i}(P_{0}\tau+{\sf b})}),

where
{\sf b}
parametrizes the B-cycle monodromy of the solution to the linear system (
B.1
).
Equivalently,
{\sf b}
may be regarded as an integration constant which depends on the choice of initial conditions for the differential equation (
B.2
), that is solved by
u(\tau)
.

At this point, the reader may treat this as a constant, and it does not play any role in the final result of this calculation.
In the same limit, we then get

(4.264)

\displaystyle v(\tau)^{2}=\left(2\pi\mathbf{i}\frac{du}{d\tau}\right)^{2}=-4\pi^{2}P_{0}^{2}-4\pi^{2}m^{2}e^{4\mathbf{i}P_{0}\pi\tau+4\mathbf{i}{\sf b}\pi}+\mathcal{O}(e^{8\pi\mathbf{i}(P_{0}\tau+{\sf b})}).

Substituting (
4.263
) and (
4.264
) into (
4.262
), we obtain that in the limit in the limit
\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty
, the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
and becomes a constant of monodromy, let us then define
\lim_{\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty}\mathcal{L}=:{\sf L}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},{\sf b})
.
Therefore, the action (
4.261
) is well-defined at
\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty
. We note that the constant
{\sf L}
will not play a role in our calculations, as will be made clear shortly.

Let us now study the action (
4.261
) near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
. We begin with the behaviour of
u(\tau)
, near its zero
u(\tau_{\star})=0
given in (
B.49
):

(4.265)

\displaystyle u(\tau)=e^{\mp\mathbf{i}\pi/4}\sqrt{\frac{m}{2\pi}}(\tau-\tau_{\star})^{1/2}\left(1\pm\frac{H_{\star}}{4\pi\mathbf{i}m}(\tau-\tau_{\star})\right)+\mathcal{O}\left((\tau-\tau_{\star})^{5/2}\right),

where
H_{\star}:=H(\tau_{\star})
is the Hamiltonian evaluated at
\tau=\tau_{\star}
.
Furthermore, using the behaviour of the Weierstrass
\wp
-function near
z=0
as specified above, we obtain the following expression near
\tau=\tau_{\star}

(4.266)

\displaystyle m^{2}\wp(2u(\tau)|\tau)=\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi m}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}\mp\frac{H_{\star}}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

From (
4.8
), near
\tau_{\star}
we have the expression:

(4.267)

\displaystyle v(\tau)^{2}=\left(2\pi\mathbf{i}\frac{du}{d\tau}\right)^{2}=\frac{i\pi m}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}\pm\frac{3H_{\star}}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

Substituting the equations (
4.267
) and (
4.266
) in the function
\mathcal{L}(\tau)
(
4.262
), we get the following expansion for the Lagrangian near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
:

(4.268)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\frac{m}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}\mp\frac{\mathbf{i}H_{\star}}{4\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

Notice that the above expression is singular near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
, implying that the action (
4.261
) is ill-defined at this point. We will now show that the term
(2-\alpha_{0})\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))
effectively regularizes the action,
i.e
,

(4.269)

\displaystyle\lim_{u(\tau)\rightarrow 0}\left(\widetilde{\phi}(2u(\tau),\tau)+(2-\alpha_{0})\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))\right)=\text{finite}.

Let us now study the expansion of
\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))
near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
. Near
z=0
, the function
\theta_{1}(z)=\theta^{\prime}(0)z+\frac{\theta^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)}{6}z^{3}+\mathcal{O}(z^{5})
. In turn, we get

(4.270)

\displaystyle\log\theta_{1}(z)=\log(\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0))+\log(z)+\frac{\theta_{1}^{\prime\prime\prime}(0)}{6\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)}z^{2}+\mathcal{O}(z^{4})

(4.271)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{def:ell-eta},\eqref{def:ell-eta-p}}\log(2\pi)+3\log\eta(\tau)+\log(z)-\eta_{1}(\tau)z^{2}+\mathcal{O}(z^{4}).

Substituting
z=2u(\tau)
and the expansion of
u(\tau)
in (
4.266
) into the equation above, we get

(4.272)

\displaystyle(2-\alpha_{0})\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))

\displaystyle=(2-\alpha_{0})\log(2\pi)+3(2-\alpha_{0})\log\eta(\tau_{\star})+\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{2}\log\left(\frac{(-2\mathbf{i}m)}{\pi}(\tau-\tau_{\star})\right)

(4.273)

\displaystyle+\frac{2\mathbf{i}m(2-\alpha_{0})\eta_{1}(\tau_{\star})(\tau-\tau_{\star})}{\pi}+\mathcal{O}\left((\tau-\tau_{\star})^{2}\right).

Let us now define the following function

(4.274)

\displaystyle{\sf F}(\tau)

\displaystyle:=(2-\alpha_{0})\partial_{\tau}\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))+6(\alpha_{0}-1)\partial_{\tau}\log\eta(q).

The above expression near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
reads

(4.275)

\displaystyle{\sf F}(\tau)=\frac{(2-\alpha_{0})}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}-\frac{\mathbf{i}\eta_{1}(\tau_{\star})(4(\alpha_{0}-2)m+3\alpha_{0})}{\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

Setting
m=(\alpha_{0}-2)
, we can write

(4.276)

\displaystyle{\sf F}(\tau)=-\frac{m}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}-\frac{\mathbf{i}\eta_{1}(\tau_{\star})(m(4m+3)+6)}{\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

Now observe that the first two terms in the RHS of the expression (
) can be re-expressed as

(4.277)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}(2u(\tau),\tau)-m\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))+6(m+1)\log\eta(q)\mathop{=}^{\eqref{Fwithm}}\int_{\mathbf{i}\infty}^{\tau}\left(\mathcal{L}(\tau)+{\sf F}(\tau)\right)d\tau^{\prime},

and near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
, substituting (
4.268
), (
4.276
) we get

(4.278)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\tau)+{\sf F}(\tau)=-\frac{\mathbf{i}(2\eta_{1}(\tau_{\star})(m(4m+3)+6)+H_{\star})}{4\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

The expression (
4.277
) is therefore well-defined near
\tau=\tau_{\star}
and (
4.269
) holds.

Finally, in the limit
\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty
, using the expressions (
A.3
), (
A.6
), we notice that
(2-\alpha_{0})\partial_{\tau}\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))+6(\alpha_{0}-1)\partial_{\tau}\log\eta(q)
is a constant that depends only on monodromy. We can therefore define
(2-\alpha_{0})\lim_{\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty}\partial_{\tau}\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))+6(\alpha_{0}-1)\lim_{\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty}\partial_{\tau}\log\eta(q)={\sf F}_{0}(\alpha_{0},P_{0},{\sf b})
. This parameter
{\sf F}_{0}
will have no role in our final computation.

Step 3: Computing
\tau
derivative of
\phi(\tau)
at
\tau=\tau_{\star}
:

We can now compute the
\tau_{\star}
derivative of the RHS of the expression (
) as follows:

(4.279)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\star}}\lim_{u(\tau)\rightarrow 0}\left(\widetilde{\phi}(2u(\tau),\tau)+(2-\alpha_{0})\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))+6(\alpha_{0}-1)\log\eta(q)\right)

(4.280)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}(\tau_{\star})+{\sf F}(\tau_{\star})+\int_{\mathbf{i}\infty}^{\tau_{\star}}d\left(4\pi\mathbf{i}\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\partial_{\tau_{\star}}u(\tau)\right)+\partial_{\tau_{\star}}{\sf F}(\tau)d\tau

(4.281)

\displaystyle\mathop{=}^{\eqref{eq:u_asymp_recall},\eqref{lag:asymp},\eqref{reg:asymp}}\pm\frac{\mathbf{i}H_{\star}}{2\pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau-\tau_{\star}).

To obtain the second line of the above expression we note that
u(\tau)
depends on
\tau_{\star}
and use the Leibniz rule. The final line of the above expression uses the expression

\partial_{\tau_{\star}}\mathsf{F}(\tau)=-\partial_{\tau}\!\left(m\,\partial_{\tau_{\star}}\log\theta_{1}\big(2u(\tau)\big)+6(m+1)\,\partial_{\tau_{\star}}\log\eta(q)\right),

which we then evaluate at
\tau=\tau_{\star}
. Finally, using the expansion of
u(\tau)
given in (
4.265
), we find the following expressions

(4.282)

\displaystyle 4\pi\mathbf{i}\partial_{\tau}u(\tau)\partial_{\tau_{\star}}u(\tau)

\displaystyle=-\frac{m}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}+\frac{3\mathbf{i}H_{\star}}{4\pi},

(4.283)

\displaystyle-m\partial_{\tau_{\star}}\log\theta_{1}(2u(\tau))

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{m}{2(\tau-\tau_{\star})}+\frac{\mathbf{i}m\eta_{1}(\tau_{\star})(4m+3)}{2\pi}\right),

which are substituted in the second line of (
4.281
) to obtain the final expression.

With (
4.281
), the
\tau
-derivative of the expression (
) at
\tau=\tau_{\star}
becomes

(4.284)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\left(\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})-\frac{2(\alpha_{0}+1)}{3}\log(2\pi)\right)\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{\star}}

(4.285)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{\star}}=\pm\frac{\mathbf{i}H(\tau_{\star})}{2\pi}.

Therefore, the accessory parameter of the Lamé equation (
4.158
) is

(4.286)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0}-2,P_{0})\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{\star}}=\mp\frac{H(\tau_{\star})}{4}.

Note that
H(\tau_{\star})\equiv H({\tau_{\star},\alpha_{0},P_{0}})
, and the factor of
1/4
arises from our choice of the central charge
c=1+6(2/\gamma+\gamma/2)^{2}
as opposed to
c=1+6(1/b+b)^{2}
elsewhere in the literature
5
5

5

It is important to note this while comparing these expressions to the results in
[
9
]
.
. This completes the proof of part (2) of Theorem
4.2
.

Remark 4.4
.

The accessory parameter has the form

(4.287)

\displaystyle\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}{\phi}(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})=\left(-\frac{\partial_{z}^{2}\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)}{\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)}+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-1\right)\wp(z)\right),

where
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is given by (
1.8
). Since
\widetilde{\Gamma}
has the explicit expression (
4.158
), the accessory parameter
\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q)
can then be written in terms of the Cameron-Martin shift and other elementary functions using the formula (
4.287
). it might be interesting to compare the expressions for the accessory parameter given by the formula above and from the
\tau
-derivative of the expression (
4.157
). We postpone this analysis for future work.

Proposition 4.4
.

The semi-classical limit of the undeformed conformal block
\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,\alpha}^{P}(q)
is related to
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
defined in (
4.249
) as the leading order behavior of the deformed conformal block with
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
in the limit
\gamma\to 0
as

(4.288)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)

\displaystyle=\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})-\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}\log q-\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}-4)}{2}\right)\log\eta(q)

(4.289)

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}+4)}{6}\log(2\pi)-\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0},P_{0}).

The parameters
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})
is a constant depending only on the monodromy data.

Proof.

Recall that the semi-classical limits of the deformed and undeformed conformal blocks in (
2.10
), (
2.18
) are related through (
2.21
):

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(\theta_{1}(z)^{\frac{\chi}{2}\left(\chi-\alpha\right)}\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi}(z,\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(\theta_{1}(z)^{\frac{\chi}{2}\left(\chi-\alpha\right)}\mathcal{W}(q)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(\theta_{1}(z)^{\frac{\chi}{2}\left(\chi-\alpha\right)}\mathcal{W}(q)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{V}_{\chi,P}^{\alpha}(z,q)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\frac{\chi}{\gamma}}\right]\right)

(4.290)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha-\chi}(q)+\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log Z^{\alpha-\chi}_{\gamma,P}+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{W}(q).

We first note that the existence of the semi-classical limit of the undeformed block is proved in Theorem
5.1
6
6

6

In the proof of Theorem
5.1
, the existence of the semi-classical limit of the undeformed block is proved in a direct way, while its uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the deformed conformal blocks and the relation (
4.290
).
. Secondly, to obtain the last line we use the fact that the limits
z\to 0
and
\gamma\to 0
for the above expression are commutative. This statement can be proved in the following way. We have an alternative description of the conformal block (
) through Girsanov’s theorem, that enables us to factor the
z
-dependence and
\gamma
-dependence. Then, one can follow the arguments of the proof of Proposition
4.2
and see that the expression for the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal block is analytic in
z
, as the
z
-dependence of the block comes entirely through trigonometric functions. It then follows that the limits commute.

Let us now study the equation (
4.290
) for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
. With the rescaling
\alpha=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma},P=\frac{P_{0}}{\gamma}
, the equation (
4.290
) becomes

(4.291)

\displaystyle\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})\mathop{=}^{\eqref{eq:subsequential_limit}}\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left(\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2}(z,\tau)\right)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}(q)+\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log Z^{\alpha_{0}}_{\gamma,P_{0}}+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{W}(q)

We now expand the last two terms in the RHS of the expression above.
Firstly, the partition function can be evaluated by using the asymptotic properties of double Gamma functions (Proposition
6.1
), and we get

(4.293)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\log q+\alpha_{0}^{2}\log\eta(q)+\mathcal{B}(P_{0},\alpha_{0}),

where
\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})
does not depend on
q
.
Secondly, from (
4.167
) we obtain the expression

(4.294)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{W}(q)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\right)\log q-\frac{\alpha_{0}(4+\alpha_{0})}{6}\log\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)

(4.295)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{24}\right)\log q-\frac{\alpha_{0}(4+\alpha_{0})}{6}\log(2\pi)-\frac{\alpha_{0}(4+\alpha_{0})}{2}\log\eta(q).

To obtain the last line of the above expression, we used the identity (
A.6
):
\theta_{1}^{\prime}(0)=2\pi\eta(q)^{3}
. Substituting (
4.293
) and (
4.295
) in the expression (
), we get the desired expression

(4.296)

\displaystyle\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})=\lim_{\gamma\rightarrow 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P_{0}}^{\alpha_{0}}(q)+\frac{P_{0}^{2}}{2}\log q+\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}-4)}{2}\log\eta(q)-\frac{\alpha_{0}(4+\alpha_{0})}{6}\log(2\pi)+\mathcal{B}(\alpha_{0},P_{0}).

∎

5.
Semi-classical limit of undeformed conformal blocks

The goal of this section is to prove our main result Theorem
1.1
, which we present towards the end of this section. We begin the section with Theorems
5.1
and
5.2
that establish the existence and analyticity of the semi-classical limit of the undeformed conformal block as a function of the modular parameter
q
respectively. We then use these theorems along with the results from the previous section to complete the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Theorem 5.1
.

The semi-classical limit
\gamma\to 0
of the one-point conformal block on the torus (
2.10
) exists for
q\in[0,1)
,
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. Specifically, the limit

(5.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(q)

exists and admits the following explicit representation. Define the function

(5.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},q):=\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]-\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right],

where
F(0;q)
and
\mathcal{Q}(q)
, are defined in (
2.2
), (
2.13
) respectively and,

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}:=\int_{0}^{1}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx.

As
\gamma\to 0
, the limit of
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},q)
is well-defined, and furthermore, the semi-classical limit of the conformal block is given by

(5.4)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(q)=-\alpha_{0}\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)+\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},q),

where

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Z}(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)

\displaystyle=\log\left(q^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}}\int^{1}_{0}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx\right)-\log\left(\int^{1}_{0}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx\right)

(5.5)

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{0}\log(q^{\frac{1}{12}}\eta(q)^{-1}).

Theorem 5.2

(Radius of Convergence)
.

There exists a constant
r_{0}>0
, independent of the parameters
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that the semi-classical limit

(5.6)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha_{0}/\gamma}_{\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(q)

exists when
q\in(0,1)
and could be extended as an analytic function of
q
for all
|q|<r_{0}
. Here,
\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
denotes the analytic extension of the torus conformal block as given in Definition
2.3
.

Corollary 5.1

(Analyticity of the Accessory Parameter)
.

For
q\in(0,1)
, the accessory parameter of Lamé equation can be expressed as

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})=\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)-\frac{\pi^{2}P_{0}^{2}}{4}-\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}-4)}{8}\right)\eta_{1}(q).

See (
4.157
) and (
4.249
) for the definition of
\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
. There exists
r_{0}>0
, independent of
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2)
and
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, such that the accessory parameter above is an analytic function of
q
for all
|q|<r_{0}
.

Proof.

The formula (
5.51
) follows from Proposition
4.4
. The analyticity of the accessory parameter
\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})
follows directly from the analyticity of the conformal block
\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)
established in Theorem
5.2
.
∎

We now proceed to prove the above two theorems.

5.1.
Proofs of Theorems
5.1
and
5.2

Proof of Theorem
5.1
.

We organize the proof into four main steps. In Step 1, we establish uniform bounds on
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},q)
using Hölder’s inequality. In Step 2, we perform the asymptotic expansion using the Cameron-Martin theorem. In Step 3, we establish the existence of the limit. In Step 4, we derive the explicit formula (
5.4
).

Step 1: Uniform Bounds via Hölder’s Inequality.

Recall the definition (
2.10
):

(5.8)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=\frac{1}{Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int^{1}_{0}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right].

Using the probabilistic expression (
2.11
), we can rewrite it as

(5.9)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=g(q)\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]}{\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(0)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]},

where

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q):=q^{\frac{\alpha\gamma}{8}}\int^{1}_{0}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx,\quad g(q)=q^{\frac{1}{12}(-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}+\alpha^{2}+1)}\eta(q)^{-\alpha^{2}-1+\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}.

With the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,
P=P_{0}/\gamma
, our goal is to show that

(5.11)

\displaystyle-\infty<\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha_{0}/\gamma)}_{\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\leq\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha_{0}/\gamma)}_{\gamma,P_{0}/\gamma}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]<\infty.

Case 1:
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,0)
.

In this case, the exponent
-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}>0
, so we need to bound positive moments of
\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)
.

For any positive integer
N
, we can write
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{N}\right]
as a Selberg-type integral:

(5.12)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{N}\right]=\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{i<j}^{N}|\theta_{1}(x_{i}-x_{j})|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}|\theta_{1}(x_{i})|^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}dx_{i}.

Noticing
\theta_{1}(z)=\mathfrak{p}(z;\tau)\sin(\pi z)
where
c_{1}\leq|\mathfrak{p}(z;\tau)|\leq c_{2}
as
\mathrm{Re}(z)
varies in
(0,1)
and
\mathrm{Im}(z)
varies in
[0,\frac{3}{4}\mathrm{Im}(\tau))
, and
q\in(0,1)
, the absolute value of the above integral is bounded above and below by constant multiples (the constant would be bounded below by
c_{2}^{-\gamma^{2}N(N-1)/4}
and above by
c_{1}^{-\gamma^{2}N(N-1)/4}
) of

(5.13)

\displaystyle\int_{[0,1]^{N}}\prod_{i<j}^{N}|\sin(\pi(x_{i}-x_{j}))|^{-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}(\sin(\pi x_{i}))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x_{i}}dx_{i}.

The above integral has an explicit expression as stated in (
2.14
) of Remark
2.2
. As
N
grows, the ratios of Gamma functions in (
2.14
) grow as
\exp(cN^{2}\gamma^{2})
.

When
\alpha_{0}<0
,
\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\right]\right|
can be bounded by
\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{N}\right]\right|
for some integer
N=\lfloor-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}\rfloor{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}+1}
. The large
N
asymptotics of the above integral (obtained through the asymptotics of the ratios of Gamma functions in (
2.14
)) shows that
\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\right]\right|
is bounded by
\exp(c/\gamma^{2})
for some constant
c=c(\alpha_{0},P_{0},r)>0
when
\alpha_{0}<0
,
P_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, and
q\in(0,1)
.

Case 2:
\alpha_{0}\in[0,2)
.

We need to bound negative moments of the GMC integral. Define
Recall that
Y(x;q)=Y(x)+F(x;q)
where
F(x;q)
is a smooth Gaussian field and furthermore,
\mathbb{E}[Y(x;q)Y(y;q)]\geq\mathbb{E}[Y(x)Y(y)]-2\log(\prod_{k\geq 1}(1+q^{k})^{2})
. By Slepian’s inequality, we get

\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q))^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}]\leq\prod_{k}(1+q^{k})^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int^{1}_{0}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}:e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}:dx\Big)^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\Big].

Applying Lemma
3.2
for
\alpha_{0}\in(0,2]
and
\gamma\in(0,1]
to this upper bound, we get

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q))^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\right]\leq\exp\left(\frac{C\alpha^{2}_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\right),

where
C>0
is a constant independent of
\alpha_{0}
and
\gamma
.
Taking logarithms and multiplying by
\gamma^{2}
:

(5.15)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q))^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\right]\leq C\alpha_{0}^{2}.

This establishes the upper bound for
\alpha_{0}>2
.

Step 2: Asymptotic Expansion via Cameron-Martin Theorem.

Following Step 1 of Theorem
4.2
, we perform a detailed asymptotic expansion of

(5.16)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q))^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}]=\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right].

We introduce auxiliary random variables
T^{(1)}_{n}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}a_{n,m}q^{nm}
and
T^{(2)}_{n}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}b_{n,m}q^{nm}
as in (
4.52
). After performing the change of variables and computing the Gaussian integrals as in Steps 1a and 1b of Theorem
4.2
, we obtain:

(5.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F_{\tau}(0)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}\right)

(5.18)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\exp\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{a}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\right)^{1/2}\right)

(5.19)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\Bigg]

Introducing the Gaussian variable
\widehat{\Psi}
in an analogous way to Step 2c in the proof of Theorem
4.1
:

(5.20)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Psi}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\sqrt{2}\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\widehat{a}_{n}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}\right)^{1/2}-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\Xi}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}Y(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)}dx,

where
\widehat{a}_{n}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}\right)
,
h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x):=\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Psi}:Y(x):]
, and
\widehat{\Xi}:=\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)}dx
.
Applying the Cameron-Martin theorem (
4.86
) we get

(5.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F_{\tau}(0)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

(5.22)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2\gamma^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}\right)e^{\frac{\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Psi}^{2}]}{2}}\widehat{\Xi}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}

(5.23)

\displaystyle\quad\times\exp\left({\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma\widehat{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)}dx}\right)

(5.24)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}}\Big],

where
d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x):=\frac{1}{\widehat{\Xi}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)}dx
. By Proposition
3.1
, the final expectation in (
5.24
) converges as
\gamma\to 0
:

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}\log\left(\int_{0}^{1}:e^{\gamma Y}:d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}\right)+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}:Y:d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}\right)\right]

(5.25)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\int_{0}^{1}:Y^{2}:d\widehat{\mu}_{0}+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{8}\left(\int_{0}^{1}:Y:d\widehat{\mu}_{0}\right)^{2}\right)\right],

where
\widehat{\mu}_{0}
is the limiting measure of
d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}
as
\gamma\to 0
.

Step 3: Existence of the Limit.

From Step 1, we have established that
\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]
is uniformly bounded as
\gamma\to 0
. Therefore, any subsequence has a convergent subsequence. To show that all subsequential limits are the same, we use the uniqueness of the asymptotic expansion show in Step 2.

Step 4: Derivation of the Explicit Formula.

From (
5.9
), we have

(5.26)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=\gamma^{2}\log g(q)+\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]-\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(0)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right].

Taking
\gamma\to 0
:

(1)

For
g(q)
: From (
5.10
) with
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
,

(5.27)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log g(q)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\left(\frac{1}{12}\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}+\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}+1\right)\log q+\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}-1+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}\right)\log\eta(q)\right)=-\alpha^{2}_{0}\log(q^{-\frac{1}{12}}\eta(q)).

(2)

For
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]
: From (
5.24
) and (
5.25
),

(5.28)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(q)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]=\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

(5.29)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm}}{n(1+2\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{2nm})}+\frac{\gamma^{2}\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\Psi}^{2}]}{2}-\alpha_{0}\log\widehat{\Xi}

(5.30)

\displaystyle\quad+\left({\frac{\gamma\alpha_{0}}{2\widehat{\Xi}}\int_{0}^{1}e^{\pi P_{0}x}(2\sin(\pi x))^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}h_{\widehat{\Psi}}(x)}dx}\right)

(5.31)

\displaystyle\quad\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\int_{0}^{1}Y(x)d\mu_{\widehat{\Psi}}}\right]

(3)

For
\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(0)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]
:

(5.32)

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\mathcal{F}^{(\alpha)}_{\gamma,P}(0)\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]=\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right].

Combining these:

(5.33)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\left(\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]-\gamma^{2}\log\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right)=\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},q).

where
\mathcal{M}_{\gamma}(P_{0},\alpha_{0},q)
is defined in (
5.2
).
Finally, the uniqueness of the semi-classical limit follows from the uniqueness of the limits of the deterministic terms in the expansion of (
5.31
) and the asymptotic expansion of the moment formulas
\mathbb{E}\big[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\alpha_{0}/\gamma^{2}}\big]
, which prove the uniqueness of the deformed conformal block. This in turn implies the uniqueness of the undeformed conformal block due to (
4.290
). This completes the proof of Theorem
5.1
.

∎

Let us now show that the semi-classical conformal block has a positive radius of convergence as stated in Theorem
5.2
. The main element of the proof is the expression of conformal block that can be obtained using the Cameron-Martin change of variable formula (Girsanov theorem)
[
24
, Theorem C.5]
such that the GMC measure is made
q
-independent. Such an expression enables analytic control over the
q
-dependent part and helps us obtain the final result.

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.

We organize the proof into four main steps. In Step 1, we establish uniform bounds (both upper and lower) on the logarithm of the conformal block, demonstrating that the semi-classical limit is well-defined. In Step 2, we perform a detailed asymptotic expansion of the undeformed conformal block using Gaussian calculus techniques. In Step 3, we carry out an analogous expansion for the deformed conformal block. Finally, in Step 4, we relate these two expansions to establish the connection with the Lamé equation and complete the proof.

Step 1: Uniform Bounds on the Semi-Classical Limit.
We begin by recalling the probabilistic formula for the analytic extension of the torus conformal block from Definition
2.3
:

(5.34)

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)=\frac{1}{Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}}\left(q^{1/6}\eta(q)\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}e^{-\frac{\alpha^{2}}{8}\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]}\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right].

The field
F(x;q)
is the smooth Gaussian field defined in (
2.2
), and the random variable
\mathcal{Q}(q)
is given by (
2.13
):

(5.35)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(q)=\exp\left(\sqrt{2}\sum_{m,n=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}\left(\tilde{\alpha}_{n,m}\tilde{\alpha}_{n}+\tilde{\beta}_{n,m}\tilde{\beta}_{n}\right)-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}\tilde{\alpha}_{n,m}\right)^{2}-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}q^{nm}\tilde{\beta}_{n,m}\right)^{2}\right).

The partition function
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
is given by (
2.11
):

(5.36)

\displaystyle Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}=q^{\frac{1}{12}\left(\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2}-1\right)}\eta(q)^{\alpha^{2}+1-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right].

Our goal in this step is to establish the following two bounds. First, we prove the upper bound:

(5.37)

\displaystyle\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]\right|<\infty

for
q\in B_{r}(0)
for some
0<r<1
. Second, we prove the lower bound:

(5.38)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\log\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha}{2}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]\right|>-\infty

for
q\in(0,1)
. In fact, we show that the above limit exists when
q\in(0,1)
and the limiting expression could be analytically extended for all
q\in B_{r}(0)
.
We observe that under the scaling
\alpha=\alpha_{0}/\gamma
and
P=P_{0}/\gamma
, the quantity
\gamma^{2}\log Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
has a well-defined limit as
\gamma\to 0
. This follows from Proposition
2.1
and the asymptotic properties of the double Gamma functions established in Proposition
6.1
. Therefore, it suffices to analyze the expectation

(5.39)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right].

Upper Bound via Hölder’s Inequality.
We first observe that
\mathcal{Q}(q)
admits the decomposition
\mathcal{Q}(q)=1+q\Theta(q)
, where
\Theta(q)
is a random variable satisfying
\mathbb{E}[|\Theta(q)|^{p_{2}}]<\infty
for any
p_{2}>1
when
0<q\ll 1
. This decomposition follows from the explicit form (
5.35
) and the fact that each term in the exponent contains at least one factor of
q
. Choose exponents
p_{1},p_{2},p_{3}\in(1,\infty)
satisfying the conjugate relation

(5.40)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{p_{1}}+\frac{1}{p_{2}}+\frac{1}{p_{3}}=1,

and such that the negative moment condition

(5.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]<\infty

is satisfied. The existence of such exponents follows from the theory of Gaussian multiplicative chaos; specifically, the negative moments of GMC measures are finite when the exponent is sufficiently small relative to
\gamma^{-2}
. Applying Hölder’s inequality with these exponents, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\Theta(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|

(5.42)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\right|^{p_{1}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}\mathbb{E}\left[|\Theta(q)|^{p_{2}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{3}}}.

We now bound each factor on the right-hand side:

(i)

First factor:
The random variable
F(0;q)
is Gaussian with variance

\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]=-4\log|q^{-1/12}\eta(q)|,

which is finite and bounded uniformly for
q
in any compact subset of
(0,1)
. Therefore, using the moment generating function of a Gaussian random variable, we have

(5.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\right|^{p_{1}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}=\exp\left(\frac{p_{1}\alpha_{0}^{2}}{8\gamma^{2}}\mathbb{E}[F(0;q)^{2}]\right)^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}\leq\exp\left(\frac{c_{1}\alpha_{0}^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)

for some constant
c_{1}=c_{1}(q)>0
that is uniformly bounded for
q\in[0,r]
for any
r\in(0,1)
.

(ii)

Second factor:
Since
\Theta(q)
involves finite linear combinations of products of Gaussian random variables, its moments are finite. We have

(5.44)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\Theta(q)|^{p_{2}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\leq e^{c_{2}p_{2}}

for some constant
c_{2}=c_{2}(q)>0
.

(iii)

Third factor:
The negative moments of the GMC integral can be computed explicitly using the following formula (derived in
[
3
]
), we have for appropriate parameter ranges

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}(\sin\pi x)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2}}e^{\pi P_{0}x}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]

(5.45)

\displaystyle\quad=e^{\frac{2\pi P_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4}-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{i(P_{0}+i)}{\gamma}+\frac{-\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(-\frac{i(P_{0}+i)}{\gamma}\right)}{\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}\left(\frac{-\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{2\gamma}\right)}\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{1-\alpha_{0}}{\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(\frac{i(P_{0}+i)}{\gamma}+\frac{-\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{2\gamma}\right)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}\left(-\frac{i(P_{0}+i)}{\gamma}-\frac{-\alpha_{0}p_{3}}{2\gamma}\right)}.

Using the asymptotic expansion of the double Gamma function from (
6.2
), which states that
\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\xi/\gamma)\leq\exp(|\psi(\xi)|/\gamma^{2})
for some function
\psi:\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
, we conclude that the right-hand side of (
5.45
) grows at most as
\exp(C/\gamma^{2})
for some constant
C=C(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q,p_{3})>0
.

Combining these three bounds with (
5.42
), we obtain

(5.46)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\Theta(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|\leq\exp\left(\frac{C^{\prime}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)

for some constant
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(\alpha_{0},P_{0},q,p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})>0
. This establishes the upper bound (
5.37
).

Lower Bound via Reverse Hölder Inequality.
For the lower bound, we apply Hölder’s inequality in the reverse direction. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}p_{3}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma p_{3}}F(0;q)}\right|^{p_{1}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{1}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathcal{Q}(q)^{-1/p_{3}}\right|^{p_{2}}\right]^{\frac{1}{p_{2}}}

(5.47)

\displaystyle\quad\times\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|^{\frac{1}{p_{3}}}.

Rearranging this inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|^{\frac{1}{p_{3}}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma p_{3}}F(0;q)}\right|^{p_{1}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{p_{1}}}

(5.48)

\displaystyle\quad\times\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathcal{Q}(q)^{-1/p_{3}}\right|^{p_{2}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}p_{3}}}\right].

Since
|e^{-F(0;q)}|
and
|\mathcal{Q}(q)^{-1}|
are exponentials of linear combinations of Gaussian random variables with finite variance, their moments are controlled. Specifically:

(5.49)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|e^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma p_{3}}F(0;q)}\right|^{p_{1}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{p_{1}}}\geq\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha_{0}^{2}p_{1}\psi_{1}}{\gamma^{2}p_{3}^{2}}\right),\qquad\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathcal{Q}(q)^{-1/p_{3}}\right|^{p_{2}}\right]^{-\frac{1}{p_{2}}}\geq\exp\left(-\frac{p_{2}\psi_{2}}{p_{3}^{2}}\right),

for some constants
\psi_{1}=\psi_{1}(q)
and
\psi_{2}=\psi_{2}(q)
that are uniformly bounded for
q\in[0,r]
. The third factor
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\alpha_{0}/(\gamma^{2}p_{3})}]
grows as
\exp(c_{3}/\gamma^{2})
for some constant
c_{3}=c_{3}(\alpha_{0},P_{0})>0
, as follows from its explicit formula in terms of double Gamma functions.
Combining these estimates, we conclude that

(5.50)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{E}\left[e^{\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\gamma}F(0;q)}\mathcal{Q}(q)\mathcal{I}_{\gamma}^{-\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\gamma^{2}}}\right]\right|\geq\exp\left(\frac{\tilde{C}}{\gamma^{2}}\right)

for some constant
\tilde{C}=\tilde{C}(\alpha_{0},P_{0},p_{1},p_{2},p_{3})\in\mathbb{R}
. This establishes the lower bound (
5.38
).

Step 2: Uniqueness and analyticity.

From Step 1, we know that
\gamma^{2}\log\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
is bounded as
\gamma\to 0
. The bounds (
5.46
) and (
5.50
) show that any subsequential limit is finite. The two key observations are that the Lamé equation (
4.158
) determines the accessory parameter
\mathcal{E}
uniquely once the solution
\widetilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
is specified, and the accessory parameter of the Lamé equation is related to the semi-classical limit of the undeformed conformal block as

(5.51)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\partial_{\tau}\phi(q;\alpha_{0},P_{0})=\frac{\pi\mathbf{i}}{2}\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}\log\mathcal{G}_{\gamma,P}^{\alpha}(q)-\frac{\pi^{2}P_{0}^{2}}{4}-\left(\frac{\alpha_{0}(\alpha_{0}-4)}{8}\right)\eta_{1}(q).

The above relation is a direct consequence of (
4.289
).
Since
\widetilde{\Gamma}
is given explicitly by (
1.8
), the uniqueness of the solution to the Lamé equation (with prescribed monodromy data) implies the uniqueness of the accessory parameter. Therefore, all subsequential limits of

\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}(q^{P_{0}^{2}/2}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q))/(4q^{P_{0}^{2}/2}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q))

must be the same, and hence the limit

(5.52)

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}(q^{P_{0}^{2}/2}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q))/(4q^{P_{0}^{2}/2}\mathcal{G}^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q))

exists for all
q\in(0,1)
. Combined with the established limit at
q=0
(from the explicit formula for
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
in terms of double Gamma functions, as shown in Proposition
6.1
), we can integrate (
5.52
) to conclude that the relation (
4.289
) exists for all
q\in(0,r_{0})
for some
r_{0}>0
. Furthermore, the limiting value which now could be expressed in terms of
\tilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
via the Lamé equation, is analytic function of
q\in B_{r_{0}}(0)
for some
r_{0}>0
thanks to analytic expression of
\tilde{\Gamma}(z;\alpha_{0},P_{0},q)
in Theorem
4.2
. Thus the semi-classical limit could be analytically extended to
B_{r_{0}}(0)
for some
r_{0}>0
. This completes the proof of Theorem
5.2
.

∎

5.2.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Theorem
5.1
proves existence of the semi-classical undeformed conformal block. Theorem
5.2
shows that the semi-classical limit can be analytically extended as a function of
q
in
B_{r}(0)
for some
r\in(0,1)
and furthermore, Corollary
5.1
shows that the semi-classical limit (as in (
4.287
)) is expressed as accessory parameter of the Lamé equation whose solution is explicitly described in (
1.8
).
This completes the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

Remark 5.1
.

The range
\alpha_{0}\in(-4,2)
in Theorem
1.1
arises from the constraints of our probabilistic approach:

•

The upper bound
\alpha_{0}<2
ensures that the GMC integral
\int_{0}^{1}|\theta_{1}(x)|^{-\alpha_{0}/2}e^{\pi P_{0}x}dx
converges, as
|\theta_{1}(x)|\sim|x|
near
x=0
.

•

The lower bound
\alpha_{0}>-4
comes from the Seiberg bound for the conformal block definition.

We expect the semi-classical limit to exist for all
\alpha_{0}\in\mathbb{C}
based on physics predictions, but our probabilistic techniques are currently limited to this range. Extending to complex
\alpha_{0}
would require different analytical methods.

6.
Asymptotics of semi-classical conformal block as
\tau\to\mathbf{i}\infty

In this section, we study the asymptotic behaviour of the deformed conformal blocks
\psi^{\alpha}_{\chi}(z,q)
(for
\chi=\frac{\gamma}{2}
and
\chi=\frac{2}{\gamma}
) and the partition function
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
(see Definition
2.2
) by setting
q\to 0
in the semi-classical limit
\gamma\to 0
. These results played a major role in Section
4
to prove the semi-classical limit of the deformed conformal blocks. In order to find the desired asymptotics of this section, a key role will be played by the representation of the partition function
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}
in terms of double Gamma functions (see Proposition
2.1
).

We find the semi-classical asymptotics of the partition function
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
in Theorem
6.1
around
q=0
. This is used in Theorem
6.2
and Theorem
6.3
to show the semi-classical asymptotics of the deformed conformal blocks
\psi^{\alpha}_{\gamma/2}(z,q)
and
\psi^{\alpha}_{2/\gamma}(z,q)
by first setting
q=0
.
In what follows, we present the ingredients for finding the asymptotics of Theorem
6.1
in few lemmas. We start by noting that the function
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma,P}(\alpha)
, which is related to the partition function
Z^{\alpha}_{\gamma,P}(q)
as shown in (
2.15
), can be expressed in the following way by comparing (
2.11
) and (
2.16
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\gamma,P}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\lim_{q\to 0}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(q^{\frac{\alpha\gamma}{8}}\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{1}(x)^{-\frac{\alpha\gamma}{2}}e^{\pi\gamma Px}e^{\frac{\gamma}{2}Y(x;q)}dx\right)^{-\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\right]

(6.1)

\displaystyle=e^{\frac{\mathbf{i}\pi\alpha^{2}}{2}}\left(\frac{\gamma}{2}\right)^{\frac{\gamma\alpha}{2}}e^{-\frac{\pi\alpha P}{2}}\Gamma\left(1-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4}\right)^{\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}}\frac{\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{2}{\gamma}+\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2}-\mathbf{i}P)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-\frac{\alpha}{2}+\mathbf{i}P)}{\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(\frac{2}{\gamma})\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-\mathbf{i}P)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q+\mathbf{i}P)\Gamma_{\frac{\gamma}{2}}(Q-\alpha)}.

We first find the asymptotics of
\mathcal{A}_{\gamma,p}(\alpha)
in Proposition
6.1
.
To this end, we first observe the following asymptotics for the double Gamma functions.

Lemma 6.1
.

For any
\xi\in\mathbb{C}
such that
\mathrm{arg}(\xi)<\pi
, we have
